# Stage 1: Vendor Procurement & Counterparty Risk — Pain Points
## Worldwide Solution-Landscape Scan — Logistics Risk Management
*Research date: 2026-05-17 | Pass 1 draft — upgraded source quality from 2026-05-14 run*

---

## Stage 1: Vendor Procurement & Counterparty Risk (Shippers, 3PLs, Freight Brokers)

**Actors:** Freight brokers, shippers with self-managed carrier networks, 3PLs and freight forwarders that onboard sub-carriers. Pains are concentrated in: counterparty identity verification at onboarding, insurance coverage confirmation, safety-record review, beneficial-ownership/sanctions screening, fraud and double-broker detection, and ongoing compliance monitoring.

---

### Pain point 1.1

**Description:** Freight brokers and 3PLs onboarding carriers into dispatch queues suffer direct financial losses when fraudulent actors — using stolen operating authority, newly minted MC numbers, or social-engineered email takeovers — pass standard vetting checks and then divert, steal, or abandon freight. Because current vetting tools are anchored to point-in-time FMCSA record checks rather than continuous behavioral signals, a carrier that passes onboarding at 9 AM can be a fraud actor by 2 PM with no intervening alert. In Q1 2026, half of all freight theft incidents involved carriers with legitimate Motor Carrier numbers and clean operating histories — evidence that passing the current vetting bar is no longer a reliable proxy for safety.

**Who bears it:** Freight brokers and 3PLs booking truckload freight on behalf of shippers; enterprise shippers with internal carrier networks; cargo insurers who fund the residual loss.

**Estimated cost:**
- Direct per-incident cargo loss (all theft types, Verisk CargoNet averages): $187,895 (2023) → $202,364 (2024) → $273,990 (2025), a 46% rise in two years (1, 2, 3). FBI IC3 PSA-260430 (April 2026) independently corroborates the 2025 CargoNet figure and identifies fictitious pickups as the dominant growth driver (4). NICB independently confirms the 2024 figure ($202,000+) and a 27% year-over-year increase (5).
- Aggregate annual cargo theft losses (US + Canada): $454.9M estimated (2024); $725M estimated (2025) — a 60% year-over-year increase (1, 2, 3).
- Annual freight payment fraud losses (distinct from physical cargo): TIA-cited range $500M–$800M+. **Deep-research outcome: confirmed-scarce.** The $800M figure originated in 2023 Anne Reinke Congressional testimony (recycled through April 2025 per Trucking Dive); no methodology disclosed; TIA State of Fraud April 2025 (N=59 members) does NOT contain the $800M figure. Treated as advocacy assertion, not auditable. **Auditable anchor for the broader cargo-fraud/theft problem: Verisk CargoNet $454.9M (2024 actual), $725M (2025 projection, +60% YoY)** — methodology disclosed, NICB and FBI IC3 cross-validation (1, 2, 6a).
- Average annual fraud loss per TIA-surveyed broker company: $402,344 (2024 survey, N=200 TIA members); per-load average: $40,760 (7).
- Prevention spend: 10% of TIA 2025 survey respondents spent over $200,000 on fraud prevention in a single six-month period (8).
- Emerging broker liability exposure: *Montgomery v. Caribe Transport II, LLC* (US Supreme Court, May 14, 2026, unanimous) held that FAAAA does not preempt state-law negligent-carrier-selection claims against brokers. Brokers who fail to document adequate carrier vetting now face exposure to tort verdicts; **per US Chamber Institute for Legal Reform (May 2024 nuclear-verdicts report, primary PDF), the 10-year median auto-accident nuclear verdict (≥$10M, 2013–2022) was $21M, rising to ~$24M in preliminary 2023 data; approximately 1 in 4 auto-accident nuclear verdicts involves a commercial trucking company.** Product-liability nuclear verdicts peaked at $36M median in 2022 — a separate case category. The only federal financial-responsibility floor for brokers is a $75,000 surety bond, which does not respond to tort judgments (9, 10, 10a).

**Willingness to pay:** Highway's Load Lock (per-load fraud protection) is adopted by 1,050+ brokers including 70 of the top 100 US brokers, providing a commercial proxy for WTP among large brokers; exact per-seat ACV is not publicly disclosed (11).

**Frequency:**
- Verisk CargoNet: 3,625 cargo theft incidents in the US and Canada in 2024, a 27% year-over-year increase; 3,594 in 2025 (stable count, losses rose 60% as criminal groups targeted higher-value shipments) (1, 2).
- TIA Watchdog: 1,600+ fraud reports in six months (September 2024–February 2025), a 65% increase over the prior comparable period (8).
- Highway blocked 527,940 fraudulent inbound emails in Q1 2026 alone (49.9% year-over-year increase); identity theft cases rose 89.6%; change-of-ownership fraud surged 169.6% with 399 ownership changes flagged as potentially fraudulent in a single quarter (12).
- TIA 2025 survey: 83% of respondents experienced at least three distinct fraud types in a six-month window; 97% identified truckload as most fraud-prone mode (8).
- DOJ prosecution: May 2025 indictment of Armenian organized crime ring (13 defendants) for stealing $83M in Amazon cargo through four fraudulently operated carrier entities — direct evidence that organized criminal enterprises are building carrier-facade structures at industrial scale (13).
- Approximately 25% of all cargo theft incidents in Q1 2026 fell into cyber-enabled categories (fictitious pickup or fraud), up from 18% of all 2024 US incidents per TT Club/BSI analysis (4, 14).

**Evidenced or assumed:**

(2) Verisk CargoNet 2024 annual analysis — https://www.verisk.com/company/newsroom/cargo-theft-surges-to-record-levels-in-2024-verisk-cargonet-analysis-reveals/

Treated as the auditable anchor that replaces the FTV/TIA single-source figure for the broader cargo-fraud/theft problem.

(11) Highway / FTV Capital press release (1,050+ brokers) — https://ftvcapital.com/insights/driving-trust-in-trucking-why-we-invested-in-highway/
(12) Highway Q1 2026 Freight Fraud Index — https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/05/05/3288141/0/en/Freight-Fraud-Hits-Record-High-in-Q1-2026-Half-of-All-Incidents-Tied-to-Carriers-With-Clean-Records.html
(13) DOJ Armenian crime ring indictment, $83M Amazon cargo theft, May 2025 — https://www.freightwaves.com/news/armenian-crime-rings-charged-with-attempted-murder-83m-amazon-cargo-theft; https://www.nbcnews.com/business/business-news/armenian-organized-crime-rings-charged-stealing-83-million-amazon-carg-rcna208222
(14) TT Club / BSI 2024 Cargo Theft Report, April 2025 — https://www.ttclub.com/news-and-resources/publications/article/bsi-consulting-and-tt-club-2024-cargo-theft-report/ (PDF processed via markitdown: 18% of all US 2024 incidents were strategic theft)

**Root Causes**

- **RC1: FMCSA registration imposes minimal identity friction, creating a low-cost entry point for fraudulent entities** — A US Motor Carrier authority (MC number) can be obtained for approximately $300 in under one week, requiring no in-person identity check and no beneficial-ownership disclosure. The structural problem is that FMCSA's registration system was designed for safety compliance administration, not for identity assurance: it was never required to link biometric identity, corporate beneficial ownership, and operating history into a single verified record. FMCSA's April 2025 rollout of biometric IDEMIA identity verification is the first structural intervention — but it applies only to new applicants, leaving the entire legacy carrier base unverified by the new standard, and does not address carrier email or contact-information takeover post-registration. FMCSA registration director Ken Riddle stated that "99.9% of everybody will be able to pass [the ID verification] if they are who they say they are," implying the prior bar was trivially defeatable. (FMCSA IDEMIA rollout: https://www.thetrucker.com/trucking-news/truckload-authority/government-affairs/fmcsas-new-motor-carrier-registration-system-is-designed-to-prevent-fraud-and-identity-theft; carrier-ok.com analysis: https://www.carrier-ok.com/post/fmcsa-s-new-id-verification-slows-motor-carrier-approvals-early-data-signals-fraud-crackdown-impac)

- **RC2: Carrier vetting is treated as a one-time onboarding event rather than a continuous identity-state signal** — Standard vetting platforms (RMIS, Carrier411, SaferWatch, DAT CarrierWatch) perform a compliance check at carrier onboarding and then monitor for regulatory-record changes (CSA score deterioration, authority lapse, insurance cancellation). They do not monitor for identity-state changes — email account takeover, phone number substitution, contact-information hijacking, or cargo diversion behavior. In Q1 2026, 50% of theft incidents involved carriers with clean operating records, meaning the threat has shifted to post-onboarding identity compromise rather than fraudulent applications — a shift that point-in-time vetting architectures cannot detect. Highway intercepted 71,801 spoofed phone calls and 527,940 fraudulent inbound emails in Q1 2026 alone, signals that no regulatory-record-monitoring platform captures. The structural cause is that the vetting industry's product architecture was built around compliance records (a static verification model), not behavioral identity assurance (a continuous inference model), because FMCSA data does not emit behavioral signals. (Highway Q1 2026: https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/05/05/3288141/0/en/Freight-Fraud-Hits-Record-High-in-Q1-2026-Half-of-All-Incidents-Tied-to-Carriers-With-Clean-Records.html; Highway 2024 fraud trends: https://highway.com/posts/2024-freight-fraud-trends)

- **RC3: Carrier fraud tools verify who books the load but cannot confirm who physically picks it up** — Most vetting platforms confirm that a carrier entity is real and authorized at the booking stage; they do not verify at pickup that the driver, tractor, and trailer match the authorized carrier profile. Double-brokering and fictitious-pickup schemes are structurally post-booking events: a legitimate carrier is booked and then either sub-contracts without authorization or never dispatches a driver at all. The DOJ's $83M Amazon cargo theft indictment (May 2025) involved four carrier entities that had legitimate FMCSA authority and contracted routes through Amazon Relay before diverting shipments — a scheme that would pass every booking-stage verification check. The structural cause is that the freight brokerage workflow creates a principal-agent gap between the entity that books the load (identifiable at onboarding) and the entity that executes it (often a sub-contracted unknown), which no booking-stage product can close without a pickup-point identity layer. (DOJ indictment: https://www.nbcnews.com/business/business-news/armenian-organized-crime-rings-charged-stealing-83-million-amazon-carg-rcna208222; FBI IC3 PSA-260430: https://www.ic3.gov/PSA/2026/PSA260430)

- **RC4: Fragmented legal accountability across shipper, broker, insurer, and carrier creates no single party with sufficient financial incentive to invest in comprehensive fraud prevention** — Under the Carmack Amendment, the carrier bears primary cargo liability, but when the carrier is fraudulent or a shell entity, recovery is nil. Under *Montgomery v. Caribe* (2026), brokers face state-law negligent-hiring claims but only when they dispatch carriers with documented safety deficiencies — the standard does not directly incentivize investment in behavioral fraud detection. Brokers' only required financial responsibility instrument is a $75,000 surety bond that does not cover tort liability. Cargo insurers pay physical loss claims but have no mechanism to recover from fraudulent carriers; they price the gap into premiums. With losses distributed across four parties and no party bearing the full expected cost, each individually under-invests in fraud prevention relative to the social optimum. *Montgomery* has begun to shift this calculus by creating broker tort exposure, but the mechanism is slow-moving litigation, not market-price signals. (Montgomery ruling: https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/25pdf/24-1238_1b7d.pdf; broker bond gap: https://beinsure.com/news/freight-brokers-face-new-insurance-gap-after-court-ruling/; TIA fraud survey: https://news.tianet.org/tia-releases-state-of-fraud-in-the-industry-april-2025-report/)

- **RC5: Criminal sophistication has outpaced FMCSA-data-anchored vetting tools, and the organized-crime threat is now international and industrialized** — The underground market for aged, clean MC numbers (available at premium prices) means fraudsters present carrier histories that pass compliance scoring. DOJ evidence shows organized crime rings build entire carrier entity portfolios: the Armenian crime ring indicted in May 2025 operated four separate registered carrier entities. Highway's Q1 2026 data shows 551 international access attempts into broker networks from India, Pakistan, and Serbia — confirming that the threat actor population is now international and organized. Criminals use AI-generated phishing, domain spoofing, SIM-swap attacks, and compromised freight-management-system credentials — attack vectors that FMCSA records have zero visibility into. No commercial vetting product currently ingests threat-intelligence signals from sources outside the FMCSA dataset. (Highway Q1 2026: https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/05/05/3288141/0/en/Freight-Fraud-Hits-Record-High-in-Q1-2026-Half-of-All-Incidents-Tied-to-Carriers-With-Clean-Records.html; FreightWaves underground MC market: https://www.freightwaves.com/news/catch-me-if-you-can-the-underground-market-for-mc-numbers-regulators-are-trying-to-stop; DOJ Armenian ring: https://www.freightwaves.com/news/armenian-crime-rings-charged-with-attempted-murder-83m-amazon-cargo-theft)

**SEA implication:** Highway detected 551 international fraud access attempts from South Asia in Q1 2026. The fraud techniques — email domain spoofing, identity theft, fictitious pickups — are infrastructure-agnostic and observed in cross-border APAC freight corridors. No SEA-native fraud-detection product currently exists. The structural conditions in SEA are more permissive: Indonesia, Thailand, and Malaysia have no FMCSA-equivalent central carrier authority registry with continuous compliance monitoring, meaning even the US-standard vetting floor is absent in those markets.

---

### Pain point 1.2

**Description:** Freight brokers and shippers onboarding carriers in Southeast Asia — and all brokers working with owner-operators below the five-truck tier anywhere outside North America — face a structural data void: no machine-readable, commercially accessible carrier authority registry equivalent to FMCSA SAFER exists in any ASEAN country. Indonesia's Kemenhub/Dishub, Thailand's DLT, and Malaysia's JPKK/LPKP each maintain national vehicle and carrier licensing records, but none exposes them through a commercial API that vetting platforms can ingest. The result is that carrier qualification in SEA defaults to document-upload and relationship-based trust — the same practices that made US freight fraud financially rational in the pre-RMIS era. The $119B ASEAN road freight market (2025 estimate) operates with zero purpose-built counterparty-risk tooling.

**Who bears it:** Enterprise shippers and multinational 3PLs managing carrier networks in Indonesia, Thailand, Malaysia, and Vietnam; digital freight platforms in SEA (Kargo Technologies, Haulio, Janio, Deliveree) that onboard sub-carriers at scale; cross-border freight forwarders routing cargo through ASEAN corridors.

**Estimated cost:**
- ASEAN road freight market size: $119.43B in 2025 (Mordor Intelligence); Indonesia alone: $53.88B (Mordor Intelligence 2025). These are commercial-vendor estimates — ADB, World Bank, ASEAN Secretariat, and ITF/OECD do not publish a USD ASEAN road freight market size (15, 15a). Used as market-opportunity sizing only, not as a cost figure.
- Manual carrier qualification in SEA: document collection, reference calls, and basic authority lookups typically require 1–4 days per carrier (with automated US tools reducing this to 30–60 minutes). At Indonesia/SEA labor rates, this is structurally a process cost borne in operational time, not a disclosed financial figure. [ASSUMED-1]: A mid-size SEA 3PL adding 20–50 carriers/month incurs approximately $4,000–$50,000 annually in manual vetting labor at coordinator-grade rates ($4–$12/hr Indonesia/Thailand) — to validate: BPS Indonesia, NSO Thailand labor force survey data for logistics coordinator grades.
- World Bank LPI 2023: Singapore ranks 1st globally; Malaysia 26th; Thailand 37th; the Philippines 47th; Vietnam 50th; Indonesia 63rd — a 37-rank spread in logistics infrastructure quality within the same trade corridor, indicating fragmented regulatory environments that make cross-border vetting uniquely complex (16).
- Cargo theft: BSI/TT Club 2024 Cargo Theft Report (processed PDF) identifies India, Indonesia, China, Vietnam, and Bangladesh as Asia's most-affected countries. The report's 2023 data showed 26% of cargo theft incidents in India, Indonesia, and Bangladesh involved insider participation — a structural risk indicator elevated by poor counterparty data availability. In 2024, Asia recorded 13% insider participation across the region, with India specifically at 26% (17).
- Indonesia road freight market is described by Mordor Intelligence as displaying a Herfindahl–Hirschman Index well below 1,000 — confirming the "thousands of small owner-operators" fragmentation structure with no centralized compliance data layer (15a).

**Willingness to pay:** Not sourced for SEA-specific vetting tooling. [ASSUMED-2]: Enterprise shippers operating in SEA would likely pay $200–$500/month for carrier vetting tooling comparable to RMIS Lite pricing, based on cost-equivalence to manual vetting labor — to validate: survey procurement leads at top-10 MNC manufacturers operating in Indonesia and Thailand.

**Frequency:**
- No vetting platform in the 20-vendor landscape reviewed in the Stage 1 value-chain map has documented SEA carrier database coverage, integration with Dishub/DLT/LPKP, or SEA fraud-detection capability (18).
- Kargo Technologies (Indonesia's largest B2B trucking platform) handles 25,000 daily loads for 5,000 shippers, yet operates in a market where carrier identity verification standards are not disclosed and no third-party vetting platform is integrated (19).
- BSI/TT Club 2024 report: Strait of Malacca sea piracy surged to a 19-year high in 2025 (ReCAAP: 107 incidents in 2024, nearly quadrupling in H1 2025 vs H1 2024), indicating that the physical security environment for goods moving through SEA is deteriorating — amplifying the risk cost of inadequate carrier counterparty verification on the road leg (20).
- OECD 2021 Competition Assessment Reviews: Logistics Sector in ASEAN explicitly identified regulatory fragmentation as a barrier to competitive logistics services and to cross-border carrier data sharing (21).

**Evidenced or assumed:**
(15) Mordor Intelligence, ASEAN Road Freight Transport Market 2025 ($119.43B, 6.04% CAGR to 2030) — https://www.mordorintelligence.com/industry-reports/asean-road-freight-transport-market
(15a) Mordor Intelligence, Indonesia Road Freight Transport Market 2025 ($53.88B, HHI below 1,000, thousands of small owner-operators) — https://www.mordorintelligence.com/industry-reports/indonesia-road-freight-transport-market
(15b) Note: no Tier-1 source (ADB, World Bank, ASEAN Secretariat, ITF/OECD) publishes a USD ASEAN road freight market size; the $119B figure is commercial-vendor consensus only.
(16) World Bank LPI 2023 — https://lpi.worldbank.org/en/home; country ranking corroboration — https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/04/21/world-bank-releases-logistics-performance-index-2023
(17) TT Club / BSI 2024 Cargo Theft Report (April 2025, processed via markitdown) — PDF: https://www.ttclub.com/fileadmin/uploads/tt-club/Documents/TT_and_BSI_annual_theft_report/TT_Club_Report_-_Supply_Chain_Report_April_2025.pdf; landing page: https://www.ttclub.com/news-and-resources/publications/article/bsi-consulting-and-tt-club-2024-cargo-theft-report/ — **Note: the 26% insider figure applies specifically to India in 2024; 2023 BSI data attributed 26% to India, Indonesia, and Bangladesh collectively. The 2024 report narrows the 26% attribution to India specifically. The Indonesia insider-theft figure is not separately disclosed for 2024.**
(18) Stage 1 value-chain map, 20-vendor SEA presence review — all vendors confirmed absent from SEA carrier data. Thailand DLT public portal (no commercial API): https://infodlt.dlt.go.th/en; Indonesia Kemenhub (no carrier API): https://kemenhub.go.id/
(19) Kargo Technologies Indonesia market presence — AirAsia/Teleport investment press release: https://newsroom.airasia.com/news/teleport-invests-in-indonesias-largest-trucking-marketplace-kargo-technologies; AsiaTechDaily profile: https://asiatechdaily.com/kargo-technologies-all-set-to-fly-high-and-rule-the-indonesian-logistics-market/
(20) ReCAAP piracy statistics 2024–2025, Strait of Malacca surge — https://www.worldports.org/piracy-in-strait-of-malacca-singapore-strait-surges-nearly-fourfold-in-2025-says-anti-piracy-watchdog/; https://shipandbunker.com/news/apac/866169-piracy-in-the-strait-of-malacca-surged-to-a-19-year-high-in-2025
(21) OECD Competition Assessment Reviews: Logistics Sector in ASEAN (2021) — https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/oecd-competition-assessment-reviews-logistics-sector-in-asean_2dcba34b-en.html

**Root Causes**

- **RC1: No ASEAN country has built a machine-readable, commercially accessible carrier authority registry equivalent to FMCSA SAFER** — FMCSA SAFER exposes the entire US carrier authority dataset via a public REST API (QCMobile), enabling private vetting platforms to build compliance monitoring products on a government-maintained data foundation. Thailand DLT, Indonesia Kemenhub/Dishub, and Malaysia JPKK each maintain national vehicle and carrier licensing databases but publish no open API and provide no commercial data-feed access for third parties. This is not a technology limitation — the data exists in each country's systems — but an institutional design decision: ASEAN transport ministries built databases for internal enforcement use, not as shared data infrastructure for private market counterparty risk management. Without a machine-readable feed, the only viable vetting approach is document-upload or manual lookup, which does not scale and cannot be monitored continuously. (Thailand DLT portal: https://infodlt.dlt.go.th/en; OECD ASEAN Logistics Assessment: https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/oecd-competition-assessment-reviews-logistics-sector-in-asean_2dcba34b-en.html; [ASSUMED-3]: Dishub/JPKK commercial API absence confirmed via inference from OECD report and absence of any commercial product built on these feeds — to validate: direct API documentation request to Kemenhub and JPKK)

- **RC2: SEA road freight is dominated by micro-fleets and owner-operators with no credit history, business registry presence, or audit trail** — Indonesia's road freight market has an HHI well below 1,000, confirming thousands of small owner-operators coexisting with a handful of integrated 3PLs (Mordor Intelligence). Without a business credit registry, formal mandatory insurance requirement published at entity level, or compliance-score publication, there is no third-party data source that could feed a risk score for these operators. The structural cause is that the informal economy structure of SEA road freight — which reduces carrier costs and serves shippers in the short run — produces a data desert that makes counterparty risk assessment at scale impossible. Kargo Technologies has converted some of this fragmentation into platform liquidity (25,000 daily loads, 5,000 shippers) but does not publish carrier compliance scores to third-party vetting platforms. (Mordor Intelligence Indonesia: https://www.mordorintelligence.com/industry-reports/indonesia-road-freight-transport-market; Kargo: https://newsroom.airasia.com/news/teleport-invests-in-indonesias-largest-trucking-marketplace-kargo-technologies)

- **RC3: US-market vetting platform vendors have no financial incentive to invest in SEA data infrastructure without a proven local revenue model** — The US carrier vetting market supports $340–$500/month SaaS products because FMCSA data is free and the fraud loss problem is quantified in public surveys. In SEA, no equivalent free data foundation exists, the fraud loss problem is not publicly quantified in credible surveys, and potential enterprise buyer counts are smaller (fewer established freight broker intermediaries with compliance budgets). Building a SEA carrier database would require proprietary data-collection agreements with government agencies and data partnerships with local freight platforms — a multi-year investment with uncertain return. The structural cause is an investment sequencing trap: the market cannot generate the revenue that would justify data investment until the data exists to make the product valuable, and no platform yet has incentive to break the deadlock. ([ASSUMED-4]: This investment sequencing diagnosis — to validate: investor interviews at AC Ventures, Monk's Hill, or Openspace about their view on logistics data plays in SEA)

- **RC4: Fragmented cross-border regulatory environments in ASEAN mean a carrier verified in one country cannot be verified against a common standard in another** — Each ASEAN country has distinct carrier permit regimes (Thailand DLT route permits, Malaysia LPKK commercial vehicle operating license, Indonesia Dishub inter-province cargo permits) that are not mutually recognized. A freight forwarder routing a load from Jakarta to Kuala Lumpur faces three separate carrier-authority verification requirements with no common data standard. The OECD's 2021 competition assessment explicitly identified regulatory fragmentation as a barrier to competitive logistics services; the ASEAN Framework Agreement on the Facilitation of Goods in Transit (AFAGIT) has not produced a unified carrier-authority data layer accessible to private platforms. (OECD ASEAN Logistics Assessment: https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/oecd-competition-assessment-reviews-logistics-sector-in-asean_2dcba34b-en.html; ASEAN domestic rules reference: https://www.asean.org/wp-content/uploads/images/Community/AEC/Sectoral/Logistic/ListofDomesticRulesandRegulationsonLogisticsandTransportServices/indonesia%20domestic%20rules%20and%20regulations%20relevant%20to%20the%20logistics%20and%20transport%20sectoral%20services.pdf)

**SEA implication:** This pain point is the primary SEA-specific opportunity. The US market analogy is pre-2005 (before RMIS/SaferWatch were built). A platform that aggregates DLT/Dishub/JPKK data into a normalized risk-score API — even at partial coverage — would be first-mover in a $119B road freight market with zero purpose-built counterparty tooling.

---

### Pain point 1.3

**Description:** Freight brokers and 3PLs onboarding even US-registered carriers face compounding vetting fatigue: a fully compliant carrier qualification requires separate lookups across FMCSA SAFER (authority status), DAT or Carrier411 (CSA score), an insurance COI verification service (RMIS or manual), a freight performance community database (FreightGuard), a fraud-signal network (Highway or TIA Watchdog), and optionally a KYB/beneficial-ownership check (LexisNexis). No single commercial product consolidates all these signal types with a single workflow. The result is that compliance teams either accept an incomplete risk picture (surface-level checks only) or bear a sustained manual-labor cost assembling a complete picture — and in both cases the assembled data is point-in-time and decays within weeks. This fragmentation problem is well-known in the industry: trade analysts describe "carrier compliance documents scattered across five different systems" as the dominant workflow complaint, and "tech overload" from too many overlapping tools as the typical brokerage condition. Post-*Montgomery* (May 2026), the documentation burden of adequate carrier vetting has become a legal imperative, not just an operational cost.

**Who bears it:** Compliance staff at freight brokers (in-house carrier onboarding and monitoring teams); operations staff at enterprise shippers with direct carrier contracting; 3PL carrier-management functions; cargo insurance underwriters assessing counterparty quality on submitted business.

**Estimated cost:**
- Manual onboarding takes 1–4 days per carrier versus 30–60 minutes for automated processes (RMIS: 80% faster than manual; one brokerage reduced onboarding from 3.5 days to under 2 hours) (22, 23). A mid-size brokerage onboarding 20–30 carriers/month with a 2–4 hour manual process per carrier (conservative estimate for fragmented multi-tool workflow) incurs approximately $25,000–$60,000 annually in pure administrative labor at market-rate US staffing costs. [ASSUMED-5]: US logistics coordinator grade loaded cost $25–$40/hr — to validate: BLS Occupational Handbook logistics coordinator compensation data.
- TIA 2024: 16% of respondents spent more than 4 hours per day on fraud prevention; 34% spent more than 2 hours per day. At the industry median company size (TIA membership is 70%+ small businesses at $1–$5M annual revenue), this represents a material operational tax (7).
- Insurance verification compliance gap: point-in-time certificate-based COI verification achieves 60–70% compliance rates versus 90%+ for real-time network-based monitoring tools — a 20–30 percentage point gap that translates to direct uncovered cargo exposure per load on a significant minority of moves (24).
- Post-*Montgomery* documentation requirement: brokers must now be prepared to produce, in discovery, timestamped records of what safety data was reviewed, whether internal vetting procedures existed, and whether red flags were ignored. Brokers without documented data-driven carrier selection processes face significantly higher insurance premiums and litigation exposure (9).

**Willingness to pay:** Demonstrated at $340–$500/month for single-purpose vetting platforms (RMIS, MyCarrierPortal). Implied WTP for an integrated multi-signal platform is higher but not publicly surveyed. [ASSUMED-6]: A fully integrated multi-signal carrier compliance dashboard would command $800–$1,500/month for mid-size brokers based on the current single-tool price plus estimated administrative labor savings — to validate: pricing sensitivity interview with compliance VPs at 5–10 freight brokers.

**Frequency:**
- RMIS database covers 97–98% of active North American carriers, yet even RMIS does not provide: behavioral fraud signals, beneficial-ownership verification, or pickup-point identity confirmation — demonstrating that the widest-coverage single tool still leaves significant signal categories uncovered (25).
- Real-time COI monitoring achieves 90%+ compliance vs. 60–70% via legacy document tools — the majority of the market remains on legacy processes (24).
- TIA 2025 survey: 97% of respondents identified truckload as most fraud-prone mode; all of them operate some variant of the fragmented multi-tool vetting workflow (8).

**Evidenced or assumed:**
(22) Foreigh.com carrier onboarding guide (1–4 days manual, 30–60 min automated; 3.5 days → 2 hours case study, 28% verification accuracy improvement) — https://foreigh.com/blog/carrier-onboarding-complete-guide-freight-brokers
(23) FreightWaves RMIS (80% faster, 98% carrier coverage) — https://www.freightwaves.com/news/rmis-automates-carrier-compliance-for-brokers-big-and-small; DAT carrier onboarding guide — https://www.dat.com/resources/carrier-onboarding-guide
(24) Insurance compliance benchmark: point-in-time vs. real-time network — [ASSUMED-7]: 60–70% vs. 90%+ compliance rates are industry practitioner estimates cited in vendor materials and insurtech commentary; cross-validation required — to validate: request compliance-rate data from RMIS and Certificial. OIP Insurtech on data silos: https://www.oipinsurtech.com/data-silos-in-insurance/
(25) RMIS 98% carrier coverage confirmed in FreightWaves: https://www.freightwaves.com/news/rmis-automates-carrier-compliance-for-brokers-big-and-small; Truckstop RMIS feature description: https://truckstop.com/blog/rmis-helps-brokers-combat-fraud/
(26) TIA 2024 fraud prevention time spent — https://news.tianet.org/key-findings-from-the-tia-fraud-report-2024/
(27) Carrier411 overview — https://www.carrier411.com/overview.cfm
(28) Carrier Assure — https://www.carrierassure.com/how-it-works
(29) FreightValidate — https://freightvalidate.com
(30) Montgomery ruling vetting documentation requirement — https://www.cottinghambutler.com/post/the-supreme-court-s-montgomery-decision-what-brokers-carriers-and-shippers-should-know; https://www.ccjdigital.com/regulations/safety-compliance/article/15825179/high-court-holds-brokers-accountable-for-hiring-unsafe-carriers

**Root Causes**

- **RC1: Six distinct signal categories are owned by different vendors with no integration incentive** — The risk signals needed for a complete carrier picture (authority status, safety score, insurance status, performance reputation, behavioral fraud signals, beneficial ownership) are maintained by separate specialist vendors: FMCSA (authority), Carrier Assure/Carrier411 (safety), RMIS/SaferWatch (insurance), Carrier411 FreightGuard (reputation), Highway/Overhaul (behavioral), LexisNexis (KYB). Each vendor's data is proprietary, each has separate API integration requirements, and each competes for the same broker compliance budget. There is no platform with first-party access to all signal types simultaneously, so integration requires either bespoke broker-side aggregation (expensive) or tolerating partial coverage (risky). The structural cause is that the market segmented into specialist verticals before a consolidation-grade platform emerged, and the largest incumbents (Descartes/Truckstop) have incentive to defend segment positions rather than enable open data exchange. (Descartes MyCarrierPortal: https://www.mycarrierportal.com/features/carrier-identify-vetting/; Highway: https://highway.com/; Carrier Assure: https://www.carrierassure.com/)

- **RC2: FMCSA data — the shared foundation — is a compliance record system, not a fraud signal system** — All commercial vetting products derive their core carrier data from FMCSA SAFER, which records authority status, insurance filings, and crash data. FMCSA does not record: who contacts a broker claiming to be a carrier, what email domain is used in rate-confirmation exchanges, behavioral anomalies in load acceptance volume, or SIM-swap events on carrier phones. These non-FMCSA signals are the primary fraud vectors in 2024–2026 (Highway Q1 2026 data confirms email fraud, spoofed calls, and ownership changes as dominant vectors). The structural cause is that the regulatory data infrastructure was built for safety enforcement, not fraud prevention — and private vendors can only add signal types they independently source and maintain, at their own cost, meaning signal coverage remains uneven and unshared across the industry. (FMCSA SAFER: https://safer.fmcsa.dot.gov/; Trucksafe chameleon carriers: https://trucksafe.com/post/chameleon-carriers-fraud-detection-and-fmcsa-s-evolving-data-strategy)

- **RC3: Insurance verification remains point-in-time and cannot reflect mid-policy changes without a real-time data network** — Carrier insurance COIs are traditionally provided at onboarding and re-verified at annual renewal; mid-year cancellations, coverage reductions, or insurer insolvencies are not automatically surfaced to the broker. RMIS provides continuous monitoring as a differentiated product, but even RMIS depends on insurers voluntarily feeding cancellation notices into the system — not all carriers or insurers participate. The structural cause is that the insurance data ecosystem is built for claims processing rather than real-time compliance broadcasting, and no regulatory mandate requires automated cancellation notice to downstream certificate-holders in machine-readable format. (RMIS: https://www.freightwaves.com/news/rmis-automates-carrier-compliance-for-brokers-big-and-small; Reliance Partners API: https://reliancepartners.com/freight-broker-insurance/api-enabled-solutions/)

- **RC4: *Montgomery v. Caribe* (2026) has created a new documentation imperative for which most brokers have no auditable workflow** — The Supreme Court's unanimous ruling means brokers must be able to demonstrate, in civil discovery, that they exercised ordinary care in carrier selection: they reviewed publicly available safety data, documented the selection process, and refused loads with red flags. A broker that selects a carrier using an informal FMCSA SAFER spot-check and no contemporaneous record faces a much weaker litigation position than one using a timestamped, automated vetting workflow. The structural cause is that the legal standard for broker due diligence has changed faster than the vetting product market can adapt: no current vetting platform generates the litigation-grade audit trail that *Montgomery* now makes commercially necessary. (Montgomery: https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/25pdf/24-1238_1b7d.pdf; broker liability analysis: https://www.mwl-law.com/scotus-broker-liability/; CCJ: https://www.ccjdigital.com/regulations/safety-compliance/article/15825179/high-court-holds-brokers-accountable-for-hiring-unsafe-carriers)

**SEA implication:** In SEA, the fragmentation problem is structurally more severe: where US brokers can at least combine FMCSA data with third-party signals, SEA brokers have no reliable first signal. The integration fatigue that US brokers feel assembling five partial signals is structurally comparable to the SEA starting position — but without even the FMCSA baseline.

---

### Pain point 1.4

**Description:** Carriers operating in markets where commercial vehicle insurance is underenforced or informal — primarily Southeast Asia, and secondarily Mexico interior and parts of Latin America — create an uncoverable verification gap for shippers and 3PLs. The workflow that defines carrier risk management for US brokers (RMIS COI verification, FMCSA insurance filing check) has no equivalent data source in SEA. A shipper or 3PL using a local sub-carrier in Surabaya, Chiang Mai, or Hanoi cannot verify that the carrier carries valid cargo liability insurance, because no third-party database of carrier insurance filings exists in any ASEAN country, insurer APIs are not public, and many carriers operate under informal fleet or group-vehicle policies not tied to an individual entity. This pain is partially supported by hypothesis WH1 — the insurance-verification gap is real — but the hypothesis overstated the acuity of the pain as it may be compensated for in practice by relationship-based trust in established business networks, reducing the immediate urgency for enterprise buyers (see Frequency note below).

**Who bears it:** Enterprise shippers sourcing last-mile and middle-mile carriers in Indonesia, Thailand, Vietnam, and Malaysia; multimodal freight forwarders with sub-carrier networks in ASEAN; cargo insurers underwriting ASEAN freight corridors without reliable counterparty data.

**Estimated cost:**
- No direct cost figure for the SEA carrier insurance verification gap is publicly available. [ASSUMED-8]: Uninsured or underinsured loss exposure in ASEAN road freight is material given average non-life insurance penetration of 1–2% of GDP in SEA versus 3–4% in developed markets — structural evidence of under-insurance — but we cannot quantify it from public data. To validate: interview cargo insurance underwriters at Marsh, Aon, or regional MGA covering ASEAN logistics corridors.
- India insider theft at 26% of incidents (TT Club/BSI 2024) and Indonesia identified as a high-risk market: when carriers are underinsured, insider-facilitated loss fully surfaces to the cargo owner with no carrier-balance-sheet recovery (17).
- US cargo insurance claims average $202,364–$273,990 per incident (2024–2025); ASEAN per-shipment exposure for electronics, pharmaceutical, and consumer goods cargo is likely comparable in magnitude but not publicly reported (1, 2).

**Willingness to pay:** Not sourced. [ASSUMED-9]: Cargo insurers underwriting ASEAN freight would pay $50–$200 per carrier verification to reduce adverse selection — to validate: interview cargo insurance underwriters at Lloyd's syndicates covering ASEAN logistics.

**Frequency:**
- BSI/TT Club 2024 (PDF confirmed): India, Indonesia, China, Vietnam, and Bangladesh are Asia's most cargo-theft-affected countries; Indonesia is explicitly named in the report's Asia hotspot list (17).
- World Bank LPI 2023: Indonesia 63rd; Vietnam 50th; Thailand 37th — logistics quality scores that correlate with inadequate regulatory enforcement infrastructure including insurance compliance (16).
- No SEA-specific carrier insurance verification platform identified in the 20-vendor scan (18).
- **Partial hypothesis test — attenuation signal:** The ASEAN freight market relies heavily on long-term shipper-carrier relationships and is dominated by established 3PLs (DHL, Kuehne+Nagel, DB Schenker) with their own carrier networks and due diligence processes. For these players, informal trust is a functional substitute for systematic verification — not ideal, but operationally adequate at their tier. The acute pain is concentrated in mid-tier shippers and the growing digital freight platform segment, not at the top-tier MNC level. The insurance gap pain may be less immediately commercially acute than PP 1.1 and 1.2 because losses stay inside established relationships rather than being routed through open load-board markets (where US fraud concentrates).

**Evidenced or assumed:**
(31) OECD ASEAN Logistics Competition Assessment: regulatory and insurance fragmentation — https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/oecd-competition-assessment-reviews-logistics-sector-in-asean_2dcba34b-en.html
(32) Reliance Partners US insurance API (context comparison) — https://reliancepartners.com/freight-broker-insurance/api-enabled-solutions/
(33) Indonesia Kemenhub licensing (no insurance API) — https://kemenhub.go.id/
(34) [ASSUMED-10]: OJK (Indonesia) and Bank Negara Malaysia do not publish carrier-level insurance status APIs — to validate: confirm with OJK and Bank Negara directly.
(35) ASEAN non-life insurance penetration comparison: [ASSUMED-11]: 1–2% GDP vs. 3–4% in developed markets — to validate: Swiss Re Sigma report or AXCO insurance data for SEA.

**Root Causes**

- **RC1: Commercial vehicle insurance in SEA is not universally tied to a carrier entity record accessible to third parties** — In the US, FMCSA insurance filing requirements mandate that carriers file insurance evidence with FMCSA, creating a government-backed insurance registry that RMIS monitors. No ASEAN country has an equivalent: Thailand DLT requires commercial vehicle insurance but does not publish a real-time active-policy database; Indonesia's OJK regulates insurers but carrier policy data is not published at entity level; Malaysia's JPKK licensing does not include insurance status in any publicly accessible data feed. The structural cause is that ASEAN insurance regulation was designed for consumer protection and insurer solvency oversight, not for B2B counterparty verification — and no commercial incentive has emerged to build the connecting infrastructure. (Thailand DLT: https://infodlt.dlt.go.th/en; OECD ASEAN logistics: https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/oecd-competition-assessment-reviews-logistics-sector-in-asean_2dcba34b-en.html; [ASSUMED-10])

- **RC2: Informal insurance structures (group policies, vehicle-fleet blanket policies) are invisible to carrier-level verification workflows** — Many owner-operators in SEA are covered under employer fleet policies, logistics association group policies, or informal self-insurance. These structures mean the carrier entity does not hold an individually identifiable, entity-linked policy that can be matched to a Dishub or DLT registration. Even a presented COI may reflect coverage that does not extend to the specific load or route being executed. The structural cause is that SEA insurance market design evolved around fleet-vehicle ownership rather than operator-entity coverage, which does not align with the verification workflow that checks whether the booking entity is covered for that specific load. ([ASSUMED-12]: Informal group insurance is prevalent in SEA commercial trucking owner-operator segment — to validate: interview insurance broker at Allianz SE Asia or Bangkok Insurance)

- **RC3: Cargo insurers in SEA price the verification gap into premiums rather than investing in data infrastructure** — In the absence of carrier insurance status data, cargo insurers underwriting ASEAN freight corridors either charge higher blanket premiums (reflecting average unknown counterparty quality), apply sub-limit exclusions for certain routes or carrier types, or decline to underwrite certain ASEAN lanes. This rational insurer response monetizes the information asymmetry rather than solving it. Because each insurer builds its own risk model from proprietary claims experience, no individual insurer has incentive to invest in shared carrier data infrastructure that benefits competitors equally — a classic public-goods problem. ([ASSUMED-13]: Insurer premium-loading as response to data gap — to validate: claims ratio analysis from cargo insurer for ASEAN vs. North American corridors)

**SEA implication:** This pain point is specific to SEA and cannot be solved by transplanting US tools (the underlying data source doesn't exist). It requires either a new data-collection layer (carrier self-certification with incentive mechanism) or an insurer-backed data consortium. This is a product-market gap that the existing 20-vendor landscape cannot fill.

---

### New pain points (not in original hypothesis)

**Pain point 1.5 — Beneficial-ownership opacity at the small-carrier tier enables organized crime infiltration that point-in-time vetting cannot detect**

**Description:** Cargo theft and freight fraud increasingly involve criminal enterprise entities — organized rings that control multiple shell carrier companies, rotate them through operating authority cycles, and use them to execute strategic theft. Current vetting platforms verify that a carrier entity is real and authorized, but do not verify who ultimately controls that entity. A carrier operated by a beneficial owner who also controls three previously suspended carriers, or who appears in adverse-media databases under a different entity name, passes FMCSA-standard vetting clean. The Corporate Transparency Act (CTA), which would have forced small US companies (including owner-operator carriers) to report beneficial owners to FinCEN, was de facto nullified for domestic entities on March 21, 2025, when FinCEN's interim final rule removed the requirement for US companies and US persons to report BOI. This regulatory reversal structurally widened the beneficial-ownership opacity gap at precisely the time when organized crime infiltration of the carrier base is accelerating — as documented by the DOJ's May 2025 indictment of a ring that operated four separate registered carrier entities to steal $83M in Amazon cargo.

**Who bears it:** Enterprise shippers and 3PLs who sustain large single-incident losses ($200,000+) from organized theft rings operating through shell carrier structures; cargo insurance underwriters modeling organized-crime exposure; mid-size brokers whose carrier networks are being infiltrated through ownership-change schemes.

**Estimated cost:**
- 22% of TIA 2025 survey respondents lost more than $200,000 in a single six-month period — the loss magnitude that typically implies organized, multi-load theft rather than opportunistic fraud (8).
- DOJ Armenian crime ring (May 2025): $83M stolen across four registered carrier entities — a named, court-documented case of the exact beneficial-ownership opacity mechanism described here (13).
- Highway Q1 2026: change-of-ownership fraud surged 169.6% year-over-year; 399 ownership changes flagged as potentially fraudulent in a single quarter (12).

**Willingness to pay:** Not sourced.

**Frequency:**
- FinCEN CTA non-enforcement for domestic entities: March 21, 2025 interim final rule — https://www.fincen.gov/news/news-releases/fincen-removes-beneficial-ownership-reporting-requirements-us-companies-and-us
- Highway Q1 2026: 399 suspicious ownership changes detected in a single quarter; change-of-ownership fraud up 169.6% year-over-year (12).
- FBI IC3 PSA-260430 explicitly identifies organized criminal groups leveraging compromised carrier accounts and identity theft — confirming the organized-crime threat vector (4).

**Evidenced or assumed:**
(8) TIA State of Fraud April 2025 — https://news.tianet.org/tia-releases-state-of-fraud-in-the-industry-april-2025-report/
(12) Highway Q1 2026 — https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/05/05/3288141/0/en/Freight-Fraud-Hits-Record-High-in-Q1-2026-Half-of-All-Incidents-Tied-to-Carriers-With-Clean-Records.html
(13) DOJ/FBI Armenian crime ring $83M Amazon cargo theft — https://www.freightwaves.com/news/armenian-crime-rings-charged-with-attempted-murder-83m-amazon-cargo-theft; https://www.nbcnews.com/business/business-news/armenian-organized-crime-rings-charged-stealing-83-million-amazon-carg-rcna208222
(36) FinCEN CTA domestic entity non-enforcement (March 21, 2025) — https://www.fincen.gov/news/news-releases/fincen-removes-beneficial-ownership-reporting-requirements-us-companies-and-us
(37) FleetOwner CTA and small carrier BOI — https://www.fleetowner.com/news/article/55260953/what-happened-with-small-carriers-beneficial-ownership-information-under-the-corporate-transparency-act
(38) FreightWaves underground MC number market — https://www.freightwaves.com/news/catch-me-if-you-can-the-underground-market-for-mc-numbers-regulators-are-trying-to-stop
(39) ICIJ Treasury non-enforcement announcement — https://icij.org/inside-icij/2025/03/treasury-department-wont-enforce-beneficial-ownership-rule-under-the-corporate-transparency-act/

**Root Causes**

- **RC1: FMCSA vetting verifies entity existence, not beneficial-owner identity** — FMCSA registration requires a legal entity name, EIN, and operating address, but does not require disclosure of the individuals who beneficially own or control the entity. A shell company with a nominee director and undisclosed principal passes FMCSA authority checks identically to a legitimate family-owned trucking company. The CTA would have partially closed this gap through a FinCEN BOI registry, but FinCEN's March 2025 interim final rule exempted all domestic entities, leaving the BOI database unpopulated for the small-carrier segment most relevant to freight fraud. The structural cause is that corporate-secrecy norms for LLC-organized businesses were designed for investor privacy, not for carrier counterparty transparency, and the one legislative attempt to change this (the CTA) has been administratively reversed for domestic entities. (FinCEN: https://www.fincen.gov/news/news-releases/fincen-removes-beneficial-ownership-reporting-requirements-us-companies-and-us; FMCSA fraud alerts: https://www.fmcsa.dot.gov/registration/fraud-alerts)

- **RC2: Commercial KYB tools are not integrated into freight vetting workflows at accessible price points** — LexisNexis Risk Solutions provides beneficial-ownership and adverse-media data that could surface shell-company patterns, but it is an enterprise license product with no FMCSA integration and no freight-specific workflow. The freight broker compliance team that needs this capability most — mid-tier brokers at $10M–$100M revenue — lacks the technical capacity and budget to integrate a custom LexisNexis workflow. The pricing gap means beneficial-ownership checks are performed only by the largest brokers (top 50–100 US brokers with dedicated legal/compliance teams) and are absent for the vast majority of the market. The structural cause is that general-purpose KYB tools were built for financial services (banks, insurance companies) and have not been productized for the freight workflow at accessible price points. (LexisNexis: https://risk.lexisnexis.com/global/en/products/lexisnexis-identity-verification-solution; FreightValidate AU10TIX integration: https://freightvalidate.com/aboutus)

- **RC3: Carrier ownership change is an event not monitored by any standard vetting platform** — When a carrier is sold, its DOT number transfers with its compliance history, and the new owner inherits the safety score of the prior entity — a feature the organized-crime market exploits by purchasing "clean" carrier authorities. FMCSA does not send real-time notifications to brokers who have worked with a carrier when that carrier's ownership changes. Highway flagged 399 suspicious ownership changes in Q1 2026 alone, but is the only commercial vetting platform that monitors ownership-change events as a fraud signal. The structural cause is that carrier ownership is treated as a corporate registry event (compliance relevant) rather than an identity-state change (fraud relevant), and the vetting industry's architecture was built around the compliance interpretation. (Highway Q1 2026: https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/05/05/3288141/0/en/Freight-Fraud-Hits-Record-High-in-Q1-2026-Half-of-All-Incidents-Tied-to-Carriers-With-Clean-Records.html; Trucksafe chameleon carriers: https://trucksafe.com/post/chameleon-carriers-fraud-detection-and-fmcsa-s-evolving-data-strategy)

**SEA implication:** Beneficial-ownership opacity is more severe in SEA, where business registry quality is lower and nominee-director structures are common in Indonesia, Thailand, and Malaysia. No equivalent of LexisNexis's beneficial-ownership data layer exists at freight-workflow price points in SEA. This gap mirrors the US problem but is harder to solve given weaker underlying registry infrastructure.

---

### Partially supported hypothesis note

WH1 hypothesized "spreadsheets and reference checks" as the dominant onboarding mechanism. Research confirms this broadly, but with nuance: at the large broker tier (top 100 US brokers), RMIS and automated tools have largely replaced spreadsheets. The spreadsheet/manual-process pain is most acute at mid-market brokers ($10M–$100M revenue) and at all tiers for non-FMCSA carriers (SEA, cross-border). The "small-transporter tier" problem is confirmed, but the insurance and beneficial-ownership sub-dimensions are more severe than the hypothesis implied, while the pure onboarding-process pain at large brokers is better addressed than hypothesized. The SEA dimension is where the hypothesis is most strongly confirmed — zero purpose-built tooling, structural data void.

---

# Stage 2: TMS, Dispatch & Freight Orchestration — Pain Points
## Worldwide Solution-Landscape Scan — Logistics Risk Management
*Research date: 2026-05-18 | Pass 1 draft — upgraded source quality from 2026-05-14 run*

---

## Stage 2: TMS, Dispatch & Freight Orchestration
**Actors:** Shipper transportation managers, 3PL operations directors, freight broker dispatchers

---

### Pain point 2.1

**Description:** Freight brokers, 3PLs, and enterprise shippers whose TMS dispatch engines auto-tender loads to approved carriers face accelerating identity-fraud exposure because dispatch-time automation is structurally in tension with per-load identity re-verification. When a load is tendered automatically to a carrier MC number already in an approved queue, no re-check of identity, insurance status, or contact details is performed at dispatch time. Bad actors exploit this by accepting a tender and immediately re-brokering the load (double brokering), harvesting commodity and shipper data, or staging a fictitious pickup. Verisk CargoNet recorded 3,625 cargo theft incidents in 2024 — a 27% year-over-year increase — with strategic theft (double brokering, fictitious pickup, identity impersonation) rising 1,475% between 2022 and 2024 and now the dominant theft vector. The TIA's April 2025 survey of its member companies (N = disclosed as multiple hundreds of brokers and 3PLs) found average annual losses of $402,344 per company and average per-load fraud cost of $40,760, with 22% of respondents losing more than $200,000 in a single six-month window.

**Who bears it:** Freight brokers and 3PLs dispatching loads through digital marketplaces and automated TMS carrier-matching; enterprise shippers whose contracted loads are double-brokered by an approved carrier; cargo insurers paying freight fraud claims; asset carriers who deliver loads and receive no payment when a double broker collapses.

**Estimated cost:**
- Average annual company loss reported by TIA member brokers/3PLs: $402,344; average per-fraudulent-load cost: $40,760 (1)
- 22% of TIA-surveyed companies lost more than $200,000 in a single six-month window (1)
- TriumphPay's combined fraud and error prevention across ~$51B in annual brokered freight: $113.58M in prevented losses from Jan 2023 through end-2024, implying a detected+blocked fraud rate of approximately 0.22% of gross freight value — undetected fraud is additive (2)
- Of the $113.58M: $26.5M specifically tied to carriers that failed Highway's Load Limit identity check (double brokering), representing the dispatch-layer fraud subset (2)
- Surety bond cost escalation for brokers driven by fraud claims: from approximately $18,000 to $40,000 in 2024 — a 122% increase in risk-transfer cost borne by the brokerage industry (3)
- Verisk CargoNet: average value per cargo theft incident in 2024 was $202,364, up from $187,895 in 2023; strategic theft incidents, which are overwhelmingly dispatch-layer fraud, are now the highest-frequency category (4)
- *Note on the $500M–$700M industry-wide freight fraud figure cited in trade press: this originates from a TriumphPay/Highway commercial press release (April 2023) and has been re-cited without independent methodological verification. No FBI IC3, FMCSA, Verisk CargoNet, Marsh, or Allianz primary dataset independently quantifies industry-wide payment fraud at this scale. Use as directional only.* [ASSUMED-1]

**Willingness to pay:** Highway Load Lock (per-load fraud protection embedded in TMS dispatch workflow) adopted by 1,050+ US brokers including 70 of the top 100 as of December 2024. Uber Freight reports 60% fraud reduction in its own network since implementing multi-layer proprietary controls in summer 2023, demonstrating demonstrated WTP among large platforms. Descartes MacroPoint FraudGuard 2.0 (launched July 2025) adds pre-tender and pre-pickup identity checks to existing TMS workflows, indicating commercial demand for dispatch-gate fraud controls. Specific ACV for these products is not publicly disclosed. (5, 6, 7)

**Frequency:**
- TIA Watchdog recorded 1,611 fraud reports in a six-month window (Sep 2024–Feb 2025), a 65% increase over the prior comparable period (1)
- Verisk CargoNet: 3,625 cargo theft incidents in 2024 (+27% YoY); strategic theft (primarily dispatch-layer fraud) rose 1,475% from 2022 to 2024 (4)
- Truckstop: fraud reports jumped 130% from 945 (2022) to 2,178 (2023) (8)
- DAT blocked 2,400 fraudulent carrier account applications from joining its network in 2023 alone (9)
- 85% of FreightWaves-surveyed freight brokers and carriers were impacted by double brokering in Q2 2023; 10% lost over $500,000 in that quarter (10)
- IDScan.net's 1M+ logistics identity-verification transaction dataset: fraud attempt rate rose from 0.53% (2023) → 1.66% (2024) → 2.15% (2025), a 306% increase over two years (11)

**Evidenced or assumed:**
(1) TIA State of Fraud in the Industry, April 2025 — https://news.tianet.org/tia-releases-state-of-fraud-in-the-industry-april-2025-report/
(2) TriumphPay $113.58M prevented losses, $26.5M Load Limit failures — https://triumph.io/blog/factor/114-million-in-potential-prevented-losses/
(3) Surety bond escalation $18K → $40K — https://transportationrecovery.com/bad-brokers-double-brokering-federal-enforcement-failures/
(4) Verisk CargoNet 2024 Annual Cargo Theft Analysis — https://www.verisk.com/company/newsroom/cargo-theft-surges-to-record-levels-in-2024-verisk-cargonet-analysis-reveals/
(5) Highway Load Lock 1,050+ brokers, 70 of top 100 — https://highway.com/posts/2024-freight-fraud-trends
(6) Uber Freight 60% fraud reduction — https://www.uberfreight.com/blog/uber-freight-doubles-down-on-fraud-prevention/
(7) Descartes MacroPoint FraudGuard 2.0 — https://www.descartes.com/resources/news/descartes-macropoint-fraudguard-provides-transportation-industry-next-generation-solution-for-freight-fraud-defense
(8) Truckstop fraud statistics — https://truckstop.com/blog/fight-freight-fraud/
(9) DAT blocked accounts 2023 — https://www.truckingdive.com/news/dat-launches-efforts-to-counter-double-brokering/686538/
(10) FreightWaves Q2 2023 double brokering survey — https://www.freightwaves.com/news/widespread-double-brokering-wreaks-havoc-on-brokers-and-carriers-in-q2
(11) IDScan.net / DC Velocity — fraud attempt rate trajectory — https://www.dcvelocity.com/supply-chain/other-services/supply-chain-security/identity-fraud-attempts-in-logistics-sector-rise-4x-over-two-years
(12) TriumphPay–Highway partnership — https://highway.com/press-releases/triumphpay-partners-with-highway-to-combat-double-brokering-fraud
(13) FMCSA double-brokering / Uber Freight transparency order — https://www.overdriveonline.com/regulations/article/15668045/fmcsa-forces-transparency-from-uber-freight-after-scam-uncovered
(14) Descartes MacroPoint FraudGuard launch April 2024 — https://www.descartes.com/resources/news/new-descartes-macropoint-capabilities-help-combat-fraud-transportation-and-logistics
(15) Strategic cargo theft surge — Trucking Info — https://www.truckinginfo.com/digital-cover-features/cargo-thefts-new-playbook-strategic-fraud-double-brokering-and-cybercrime-hit-trucking

**Root Causes**

- **RC1: Automated dispatch workflows treat carrier MC-number approval as a persistent credential rather than a per-load identity assertion** — Enterprise TMS and digital freight marketplace dispatch engines are architected to maximize booking speed: once a carrier is in an approved list, loads are tendered automatically without re-checking identity, insurance status, or contact information at dispatch time. This is a deliberate design choice — re-verification at every tender event degrades throughput. The structural result is a persistent gap between the identity state verified at onboarding and the identity state at the moment of physical pickup. Highway detected 109,672 FMCSA contact changes in 2024 that were potential fraud signals, most occurring after carriers had been onboarded. No TMS vendor has built dispatch-time re-verification as a default workflow element; the three products approaching it (Highway Load Lock, Uber Freight's fraud layer, Descartes MacroPoint FraudGuard 2.0) are add-on integrations, not native dispatch-gate functions. (Highway 2024 Fraud Trends: https://highway.com/posts/2024-freight-fraud-trends; Descartes MacroPoint announcement: https://www.descartes.com/resources/news/new-descartes-macropoint-capabilities-help-combat-fraud-transportation-and-logistics)

- **RC2: Digital freight marketplaces compete on booking automation speed, creating a commercial disincentive to add friction at the dispatch stage** — Platform take rates of 10–20% on spot FTL freight are achievable only through high booking velocity. Adding identity re-verification steps increases booking latency, degrades carrier experience, and reduces platform utilization — all of which reduce revenue per engineering dollar. The structural cause is that fraud risk is externalized: the shipper or cargo insurer bears the cargo loss, while the platform retains its take rate regardless of whether the carrier was fraudulent. Uber Freight's FY2024 EBITDA margin was (1.7%) on $1.28B gross bookings; margin recovery requires increasing load volumes, not reducing them through additional friction gates. Until platforms bear the full cost of fraud losses (rather than FMCSA-mandated disclosure only), the automation-versus-verification trade-off resolves in favor of automation. (Uber Freight FY2024: https://investor.uber.com/news-events/news/press-release-details/2025/Uber-Announces-Results-for-Fourth-Quarter-and-Full-Year-2024/default.aspx)

- **RC3: Spot-mode loads routed through TMS carry contracted-grade trust assumptions but sub-contracted-grade vetting rigor** — TMS dispatch engines typically maintain two carrier pools: contracted carriers (vetted, EDI-integrated, performance-tracked) and spot carriers (ad hoc, booked via load board, lighter compliance check). When contract capacity fails — outbound tender rejection rates exceeded 13% at the late-2025 peak season — overflow loads route to the spot pool without triggering a higher-risk-threshold identity check. The Acocella/Caplice (2023) MIT/JBL review of truckload transportation procurement identifies this structural two-stage gap: strategic contracts are binding in price but not in capacity or carrier compliance at execution time, and the execution "buy stage" is under-researched and under-controlled. Spot-carrier fraud rates are structurally higher because spot pools include newly registered MCs and load-board ad-hoc operators. [ASSUMED-2]: No primary survey has quantified the fraud-rate differential between spot and contracted carrier pools within a single TMS; the structural logic is supported by Verisk CargoNet's strategic theft surge data and industry practitioner testimony. — to validate: Ask a freight broker operating a hybrid contracted/spot TMS to pull fraud incident data by carrier pool type for the last 12 months. (Acocella & Caplice, JBL 2023: https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/jbl.12333; spot tender rejection rate: https://blog.gettransport.com/news/spot-market-tender-rejection-rates/)

- **RC4: No industry standard governs minimum fraud-prevention capability in a TMS dispatch workflow, leaving fraud risk a function of vendor investment choices** — The TMS market (Gartner MQ 2024 Leaders: Oracle OTM, e2open, Manhattan Active TM, Blue Yonder, SAP TM) is evaluated on optimization quality, integration breadth, and usability — not on fraud prevention. Gartner's evaluation criteria for TMS include strategic freight sourcing, carrier selection, execution, analytics, and settlement — but not carrier identity fraud prevention as a scored capability. Fraud controls are optional modules or third-party integrations (RMIS, Highway, Carrier411), not audited baseline requirements. The TIA's 2024 Framework to Combat Fraud is voluntary, with zero compliance enforcement. Until a minimum-capability standard is enforced, incumbents with large installed bases face no market pressure to invest in fraud controls. (Gartner MQ TMS 2024: https://www.e2open.com/news/press-releases/e2open-again-positioned-as-a-leader-in-2024-gartner-magic-quadrant-for-transportation-management-systems/; TIA 2024 Framework: https://news.tianet.org/2024-tia-framework-to-combat-fraud/)

- **RC5: FMCSA's $75,000 broker surety bond requirement is structurally inadequate to protect carriers and shippers when large platforms fail, removing a financial incentive for platforms to self-police dispatch-layer fraud risk** — The $75,000 minimum surety bond was last revised in 2012 and was designed for small freight brokers, not billion-dollar digital platforms aggregating thousands of carrier payment obligations. Convoy was managing $750M+ in annual freight throughput at peak while carrying only $75,000 in bonded coverage — a coverage ratio of 0.01%. The IKEA interpleader showed carriers holding $500,000+ in disputed invoices had no meaningful recourse against the surety. FMCSA's January 2026 rule changes tighten enforcement of the existing $75,000 floor but do not increase the amount. The structural cause: until bonding scales with platform freight throughput, platforms face no financial liability incentive to invest in dispatch-layer fraud prevention. (FMCSA 2026 bond enforcement: https://www.freightwaves.com/news/fmcsas-tighter-bond-enforcement-looms-over-freight-brokers-in-2026; IKEA interpleader: https://sourcingjournal.com/topics/logistics/ikea-sues-convoy-solve-500000-dispute-unpaid-trucking-carriers-lawsuit-freight-brokerage-interpleader-truckers-497146/)

**SEA implication:** SEA digital freight platforms (Kargo Technologies — 75,000+ trucks in network, $51.56M raised; Haulio; Deliveree; GoGoX) operate predominantly in spot-mode dispatch with app-based carrier matching. Their driver/carrier pools are dominated by micro-fleets (Indonesia's logistics market fragmentation is documented: logistics costs equal **14.29% of GDP** per the 2023 Australia–Indonesia Partnership survey cited by WCO News, primary source; older non-primary citations report 24% but the WCO-cited figure is the authoritative recent number). Fragmented carrier ownership is identified as a structural constraint. There is no FMCSA-equivalent authority database to validate carrier identity against. The fraud vectors that have materialized in US digital freight markets exist structurally in SEA but without even the partial mitigations that Highway and TriumphPay provide. [ASSUMED-3]: SEA digital freight platforms have not published fraud incident statistics; the SEA structural analysis rests on market fragmentation data and inference from US analog. — to validate: Interview a compliance officer at Kargo Technologies about their carrier identity verification process at dispatch.

### Pain point 2.2

**Description:** Mid-market shippers — those with annual freight spend roughly between $10M and $100M — face a structural technology gap: enterprise TMS (SAP TM, Oracle OTM, Blue Yonder, e2open) is priced and implemented at a scale they cannot justify, while spreadsheet-and-email workflows cannot handle the complexity, compliance requirements, or volume they generate. This leaves them in a bandwidth and risk-management dead zone: too large to run freight operations informally, too small to absorb $2M–$6M implementation costs plus dedicated IT and TMS staff. InMotion Global's TMS adoption survey found that TL shipper TMS adoption is approximately 51% among those tendering ~40 loads/week, with adoption falling sharply below that volume threshold; companies with freight spend between $25M and $100M show approximately 25% TMS adoption vs. 50%+ for companies over $100M. The consequence is not just operational inefficiency: shippers operating on spreadsheets have no systematic carrier vetting, no routing-guide discipline, no automated compliance monitoring, and no audit trail — leaving them entirely exposed to carrier fraud, cargo claims, and regulatory non-compliance without the tools to detect any of these risks.

**Who bears it:** Shipper transportation managers at mid-market manufacturers, distributors, and retailers with $10M–$100M annual freight spend; 3PL operations directors managing mid-market clients without TMS integration; freight brokers serving mid-market shippers that must compensate for their client's manual workflows.

**Estimated cost:**
- Mid-market TMS implementations: $150K–$400K before optimization, plus $50K–$150K annual licensing; first-year total cost $300K–$720K; annual operating cost $200K–$400K (license, staffing, IT support) [ASSUMED-4] *[Nuvocargo-cited ranges, confirmed directionally by transportmanagement.org and selecthub.com; enterprise SI interview data not available publicly.]*
- Enterprise TMS license + implementation for large shippers: $1M–$5M+ annual license, $2M–$6M year-1 professional services for SAP TM/Oracle OTM [ASSUMED-5] *[Gartner Critical Capabilities for TMS and Forrester TEI are paywalled — tagged [NEEDS-ATTENDED-FETCH]. Directionally confirmed by: noeldcosta.com SAP TM implementation breakdown; selecthub.com; the one public datapoint is a $1M/year SAP TM for a $30M-freight-spend shipper.]* (16, 17)
- Industry freight invoice error rate: 3–7% of annual freight spend is widely cited (vendor marketing); **deep-research outcome: confirmed-scarce** — Cass Information Systems, U.S. Bank Freight Payment Index, AFS Logistics publish no public quantified freight invoice error rate; the only adjacent primary data is Ardent Partners AP Metrics 2024 (n=190) at 20.7% all-sector invoice exception rate (broader than freight). The 3–7% figure is treated as [ASSUMED] until APQC primary or CSCMP full-report figure is obtained via attended-fetch. (18, 18a)
- Tender rejection premium: when contract carriers reject a load and it spills to spot, shippers pay an average 14.8% spot premium per rejected load (19); mid-market shippers without TMS routing guides cannot enforce first-tender sequences or track rejections systematically, meaning they overpay spot premiums at higher rates than TMS users
- Companies with $2M–$10M freight spend using TMS report using only 30–40% of TMS capabilities, suggesting even those who invest do not fully capture risk-management value (20)
- 43% of TMS users cite operational complexity as their top frustration (Logistics Management Annual Survey 2025, via Nuvocargo) (20)

**Willingness to pay:** Not sourced at a primary level for this cohort. [NEEDS-ATTENDED-FETCH]: Gartner Critical Capabilities for TMS 2024 includes mid-market use case scoring that may contain WTP data.

**Frequency:**
- Overall TMS adoption among logistics professionals: 61% (InMotion Global TMS survey, n=not disclosed, cited in FleetOwner) (21)
- TL shipper TMS adoption: ~51% among those tendering ~40 loads/week; falls sharply below that threshold; companies with $25M–$100M freight spend: ~25% TMS adoption vs. 50%+ for $100M+ (21)
- LTL shipper TMS adoption: 64% for shippers with annual freight spend over $500K (vs. 27% in 2015) — indicating cloud TMS has grown adoption but absolute penetration is still below majority for smaller-spend cohorts (21)
- 76% of enterprise TMS migrations "fail to meet their objectives" per European cloud TMS migration analysis (22)
- 9–18 months to reach full operational capability vs. 3–6 months commonly projected (Gartner 2024, cited in Nuvocargo) (20)
- US TMS market: $4.8B (2024), growing at CAGR ~17.6% to $38B by 2030 — growth driven substantially by mid-market cloud TMS adoption, confirming this is an active market gap being addressed but not yet closed (23)

**Evidenced or assumed:**
(16) SAP TM implementation: https://noeldcosta.com/sap-implementation-cost-and-budget-breakdown/; https://www.selecthub.com/tms-software/oracle-transportation-management-vs-sap-transportation-management/
(17) Blue Yonder TMS implementation analysis — https://locus.sh/blogs/blue-yonder-pricing/

Not freight-specific but provides primary anchor for invoice exception magnitudes.
