**Root Causes**

- **RC1: Enterprise TMS platforms are priced and architected for global multi-modal complexity that mid-market shippers do not need, while their minimum viable feature set is still priced at enterprise scale** — SAP TM and Oracle OTM have been built over decades for the needs of $1B+ freight spenders managing multi-modal, multi-country, multi-ERP environments. Their minimum deployable configuration still requires ERP integration, a dedicated TMS administrator, and SI professional services that price out at $300K–$720K in year-1 for a shipper with $20M–$50M freight spend. The structural cause is that the development economics of enterprise software reward adding features (which justify enterprise pricing) rather than simplifying for smaller buyers. Cloud TMS entrants (Cargobase, Flexport TMS, Loadsmart) have partially addressed this but have not replicated the multi-modal carrier compliance depth of enterprise platforms. The mid-market buyer remains structurally squeezed between price and capability. (ARK TMS analysis: https://arktms.com/blog/broker-growth-opportunity; transportmanagement.org mid-market analysis: https://www.transportmanagement.org/the-european-mid-market-tms-adoption-crisis-how-to-overcome-the-5-critical-barriers-preventing-implementation-success-before-vendor-consolidation-and-regulatory-deadlines-eliminate-your/)

- **RC2: Spreadsheet-based freight workflows have no native mechanism to enforce carrier vetting compliance, creating a risk-management blind spot that scales with freight volume** — A mid-market shipper managing 30–150 loads per week on spreadsheets makes carrier selection decisions based on relationship memory, stored rate quotes, and phone calls — with no systematic check of FMCSA safety ratings, insurance currency, or compliance monitoring alerts. Carrier411 documents that insurance lapses and FMCSA rating downgrades are continuous events in the carrier population; without a TMS-integrated monitoring feed, a spreadsheet operator has no alert mechanism. The structural cause is that spreadsheets are a universal coordination tool with no built-in supply-chain compliance layer, and mid-market shippers have no budget for standalone compliance monitoring subscriptions that enterprise users embed in TMS. (Carrier411 monitoring: https://www.carrier411.com/carrier-monitoring-service.cfm; locus.sh carrier management: https://locus.sh/blogs/carrier-management/)

- **RC3: Manual workflows persist inside TMS-deploying organizations because TMS systems automate scheduling mechanics but not judgment-heavy exception management and carrier relationship activities** — Even among the 51% of mid-to-large shippers with TMS, 43% cite operational complexity as their top frustration, and companies use only 30–40% of TMS capabilities (Logistics Management 2025, Gartner, cited in Nuvocargo). The specific functions that remain manual — carrier relationship management, exception escalation, invoice dispute resolution, performance accountability conversations — are the functions most directly tied to risk management. The structural cause is that TMS vendors competed historically on optimization algorithms and ERP integration, not on workflow automation for exception handling. A shipper can auto-tender 80% of loads but still requires a dispatcher to manually intervene on every carrier rejection, every compliance exception, and every disputed invoice — consuming the labor capacity that was supposed to be freed by the TMS. (Nuvocargo TMS analysis: https://www.nuvocargo.com/blog-posts/why-your-tms-isnt-working; Drumkit workflow analysis: https://www.drumkit.ai/blog/the-evolution-of-load-management-from-manual-to-automated-with-freight-broker-software)

- **RC4: Mid-market TMS implementations fail at a high rate, destroying the investment case and leaving shippers worse off than pre-implementation** — 76% of European cloud TMS migrations fail to meet their objectives; 9–18 months to reach full operational capability vs. 3–6 months projected. The structural cause is a combination: (a) mid-market shippers lack dedicated IT resources to manage ERP integrations that are a core dependency of full TMS function; (b) carrier EDI onboarding requires per-carrier configuration that is labor-intensive and often falls to the shipper's logistics staff rather than the TMS vendor; (c) data migration from spreadsheets introduces data quality issues (stale carrier records, inconsistent lane rates) that degrade system performance for months post-go-live. The consequence is that failed implementations don't just waste $300K–$720K — they create a carrier data environment that is worse than the spreadsheet it replaced, because the TMS's approved-carrier list may contain records that are months out of date. (76% failure: https://www.transportmanagement.org/the-european-shippers-cloud-tms-migration-guide-how-to-navigate-2025s-shift-from-on-premise-without-joining-the-76-failure-rate/; Nuvocargo: https://www.nuvocargo.com/blog-posts/why-your-tms-isnt-working)

**SEA implication:** TMS adoption among mid-market SEA shippers is structurally lower than in North America and Europe. Traditional TMS solutions are identified as routinely falling short in addressing SEA's diverse geography, dense urban traffic, and variable infrastructure. The fragmentation of Indonesia's logistics market (logistics costs = **14.29% of GDP** per WCO 2024 citing 2023 Australia–Indonesia Partnership survey, primary; still high by global standards; carrier ownership dominated by micro-fleets) means the average SEA mid-market shipper manages a more complex carrier pool than a comparable US shipper — but with less technology and no FMCSA-equivalent compliance data to draw from. [ASSUMED-6]: Primary survey data on SEA mid-market TMS adoption rates does not exist in public form; the SEA analysis rests on market fragmentation reports and inference from global adoption patterns. — to validate: Survey transportation managers at 20 mid-market manufacturers in Indonesia and Thailand about their current carrier management process.

### Pain point 2.3

**Description:** Enterprise shippers and 3PLs that tender loads to sub-contracted carriers through a 3PL intermediary face a risk-context preservation failure at the handoff: the shipper's TMS records the load as tendered to the 3PL, but the risk attributes attached to that load — cargo value, special handling requirements, temperature sensitivity, regulatory compliance obligations, high-value commodity classification — are not systematically transmitted when the 3PL re-tenders to its own carrier network. The shipper's TMS sees "tendered to 3PL" and stops; it has no visibility into which sub-carrier physically moves the freight, what that carrier's safety rating or compliance status is, or whether the 3PL applied the shipper's risk criteria when selecting the sub-carrier. Cargo theft incidents increased 93% between 2021 and 2024 overall; strategic cargo theft (which exploits exactly this handoff gap) rose 1,475% in the same period. The Marsh Marine, Cargo and Logistics Trends reports consistently identify subcontractor chain risk as a leading contributor to uninsured cargo loss exposure.

**Who bears it:** Enterprise shippers whose cargo moves through 3PL-managed carrier networks; 3PLs liable for cargo losses caused by sub-carriers they selected without shipper-approved criteria; cargo insurers pricing policies for shippers without visibility into actual carrier execution chains; factoring companies advancing against 3PL invoices for loads that were subsequently mishandled.

**Estimated cost:**
- CargoNet 2024: 3,625 cargo theft incidents, average value $202,364 per incident; Q1 2024 cargo theft jumped 46% year-over-year — the highest quarterly spike on record (26)
- LTL damage rate: up to 3.5% of LTL freight damaged in transit, with a significant share occurring at inter-carrier handoffs (27)
- 3PL freight claim processing cost: claims involving disputed sub-carrier liability require legal review, evidence collection, and negotiation — a mid-size 3PL handling 50 cargo claims per year at $5,000–$15,000 in processing cost per claim incurs $250K–$750K in claims management overhead annually, excluding the claim payments themselves [ASSUMED-7]
- Compliance monitoring gap: "carriers with lapsed insurance certificates keep moving freight until someone manually catches it" — with no automated sub-carrier monitoring feed from 3PL to shipper TMS, insurance lapses at the sub-carrier level generate uninsured exposure that surfaces only at claim time (28)
- WH2 (from input hypotheses): shipper TMS sees the load tendered to 3PL but not what the 3PL does after. This is **confirmed as structurally real** by industry sources: "Handing freight tendering off to a 3PL gives you little control over who your cargo is given to, limiting your ability to weed out underperformers." The pain is real; the prior hypothesis that it is a dominant pain point is partially modified — it is a real structural gap but one that is addressed at contracting time by sophisticated enterprise shippers (carrier approval clauses in 3PL contracts), meaning the pain is concentrated in mid-market shippers and 3PLs without formal subcontractor governance frameworks.

**Willingness to pay:** Not sourced at a primary level for sub-carrier visibility specifically.

**Frequency:**
- Cargo theft incidents rose 93% from 2021 to 2024; strategic theft (which exploits handoff gaps) rose 1,475% (26)
- CargoNet Q3 2024: cargo theft "held steady" at elevated levels, with organized crime increasingly targeting specific commodity types through sub-carrier network infiltration (29)
- Supply chain 3PL risk survey (Risk Strategies 2024): without direct control over contractors' coverage, "3PLs sometimes make a calculated business decision to contract with smaller deliverers lacking proper coverage" — confirmed as an industry practice norm (30)
- 3PL Perspectives Report 2024 (FreightCaviar, survey of 3PL operators): technology and compliance gaps identified as top operational challenges, with sub-carrier visibility cited as a recurring issue in practitioner commentary (31)

**Evidenced or assumed:**
(26) Verisk CargoNet 2024 Annual Analysis — https://www.verisk.com/company/newsroom/cargo-theft-surges-to-record-levels-in-2024-verisk-cargonet-analysis-reveals/
(27) LTL damage rate 3.5% — https://www.tjocargo.com/the-abcs-of-ground-cargo-risk-mitigation; https://www.accio.com/business/freight_claims_trends
(28) Locus carrier management — insurance lapse gap — https://locus.sh/blogs/carrier-management/
(29) CargoNet Q3 2024 analysis — https://www.cargonet.com/news-and-events/cargonet-in-the-media/2024-q3-theft-trends/
(30) Risk Strategies 3PL compliance — https://www.risk-strategies.com/blog/compliance-challenges-and-opportunities-for-3pls
(31) 3PL Perspectives Report 2024 — https://www.freightcaviar.com/3pl-perspectives-report-2024-tough-times-tech-trends/
(32) TransLogistics 3PL visibility gap — https://www.translogisticsinc.com/blog/3pl-visibility
(33) FreightWaves 3PL compliance questions — https://www.freightwaves.com/news/7-compliance-and-safety-questions-to-ask-your-freight-broker-and-3pl-partners
(34) Supply Chain Management Review — freight market risk signals — https://www.scmr.com/article/the-freight-markets-new-reality-more-risk-fewer-signals/procurement
(35) Marsh 3PL liability risks — https://www.marsh.com/en/industries/cargo/insights/third-party-logistics.html

**Root Causes**

- **RC2 [hypothesis partially modified]:** The prior hypothesis (WH2) framed this as a universal pain at the TMS orchestration layer. Research shows it is real but structurally concentrated: large shippers with sophisticated procurement teams negotiate carrier-approval clauses and compliance requirements into 3PL master service agreements, partially mitigating the visibility gap at contracting time. The residual pain is concentrated in (a) mid-market shippers without 3PL contracting sophistication, (b) 3PLs tendering to informal sub-carrier pools in markets without FMCSA-equivalent registries (SEA), and (c) cases where approved sub-carriers themselves sub-contract (double sub-contracting), which no contracting clause systematically prevents.

- **RC1: TMS tender-to-3PL events do not generate a downstream compliance tracking obligation in the shipper's system** — When a shipper TMS auto-tenders a load to a 3PL, the dispatch event is recorded as complete. No native TMS workflow creates a linked compliance obligation to verify which sub-carrier the 3PL assigned, what that carrier's safety rating is, or whether the 3PL applied the shipper's approved-carrier criteria. The structural cause is that TMS data models were designed around the shipper-carrier relationship, not the shipper-3PL-sub-carrier three-tier chain. Adding sub-carrier traceability requires an API connection between the 3PL's TMS and the shipper's TMS — an integration that exists in EDI-heavy enterprise environments but is absent in mid-market and SEA contexts. (locus.sh carrier management: https://locus.sh/blogs/carrier-management/; translogisticsinc.com 3PL visibility: https://www.translogisticsinc.com/blog/3pl-visibility/)

- **RC2: 3PLs have a commercial incentive to protect their carrier network information as proprietary, reducing transparency to shipper TMS systems** — A 3PL's carrier network — the relationships, rates, and routing knowledge embedded in its sub-carrier pool — is a core competitive asset. Sharing real-time sub-carrier identity with the shipper's TMS creates risk that the shipper will bypass the 3PL and contract directly with the carrier on future loads. This commercial dynamic creates a structural disincentive for 3PLs to expose their sub-carrier assignment data to shipper-facing visibility layers. The result is that shippers who want sub-carrier visibility must negotiate it into contracts at considerable effort, and many do not — particularly mid-market shippers without dedicated procurement legal resources. [ASSUMED-8]: The extent to which 3PLs actively resist sub-carrier transparency vs. simply lacking the technical capability to provide it has not been surveyed. — to validate: Interview 3PL operations directors at three mid-size 3PLs about whether sub-carrier visibility is a client request they receive, and how they respond.

- **RC3: The cargo insurance liability chain is ambiguous when a sub-carrier causes loss, creating post-incident disputes that persist because the shipper's TMS has no record of which carrier actually moved the freight** — When cargo is damaged or stolen and the shipper's TMS shows only "tendered to 3PL," the insurance claim requires establishing which physical carrier had possession at the time of loss. Without TMS records of sub-carrier assignment, the claim process relies on 3PL-provided documentation, which may be incomplete, delayed, or contested. The structural cause is that cargo insurance underwriting and claims processes were designed for direct shipper-carrier relationships, and the multi-tier 3PL chain creates an evidence gap that inflates claims processing time and dispute rates. Risk Strategies (2024) identifies sub-contractor coverage gaps as a leading 3PL liability exposure. (Risk Strategies: https://www.risk-strategies.com/blog/compliance-challenges-and-opportunities-for-3pls; Marsh 3PL: https://www.marsh.com/en/industries/cargo/insights/third-party-logistics.html)

- **RC4: In SEA markets, sub-carrier chains are longer, more informal, and entirely outside any registry system, making sub-carrier risk invisible to both 3PL and shipper TMS** — In Indonesia, Philippines, and Thailand, the trucking market is dominated by micro-fleets where a "carrier" is often a single owner-operator who may in turn hire a freelance driver for a specific run. The concept of a registered, insured, compliance-tracked carrier network that a 3PL can hold to a standard is absent for most of the market. No FMCSA-equivalent registry tracks carrier authority, insurance, or safety rating in these markets. Kargo Technologies (75,000+ trucks, $51.56M raised) explicitly positions itself on carrier vetting and reputation systems as a differentiator — confirming that unvetted sub-carrier dispatch is the status quo norm it is competing against. [ASSUMED-9]: The proportion of SEA freight moved through informal sub-carrier chains with no formal compliance tracking is not quantified in public primary research. — to validate: Interview a cargo insurance underwriter specializing in SEA trucking about how they price policies where sub-carrier chains are informal.

**SEA implication:** The structural pain is amplified in SEA by the complete absence of a carrier registry, combined with the dominance of spot-mode dispatch through digital platforms. A shipper in Indonesia tendering to Kargo or a local 3PL has no systematic mechanism to know which micro-fleet owner-operator ultimately moves their cargo. This is both a fraud risk and a cargo damage risk, with no claim evidence trail when incidents occur.

---

### Pain point 2.4

**Description:** Freight brokers, 3PLs, and shippers using TMS freight procurement modules face a structural disconnect between the strategic carrier selection performed at contract award and the risk-informed dispatch decision that should be made at the load-execution stage. Carrier performance data — safety scores, on-time delivery rates, cargo claim rates, recent compliance changes — exists in separate systems (FMCSA, ELD networks, TMS performance modules, RMIS) but does not feed back into routing guide priority or automated tender sequences in real time. A carrier who scored 94% on-time in Q1 may be at 71% by Q3 due to driver turnover or fleet disposition changes, yet the TMS routing guide still sends them priority volume because no automated cadence updates the allocation rule. Acocella & Caplice (JBL 2023, MIT Center for Transportation and Logistics) establish this as a documented structural gap: the academic literature on truckload procurement largely treats strategic contracting and execution-stage dispatch as separate problems, with the execution "buy stage" under-researched and rarely incorporated into procurement optimization models. Empirically: a significant share of FTL contracts — on average 70% in some market conditions — go unused (volume not tendered as contracted), and the additional cost when carriers reject loads has been estimated at 10–30% depending on market conditions.

**Who bears it:** Shipper transportation managers who manage routing guides; 3PL operations directors responsible for carrier allocation efficiency; freight brokers managing hybrid contracted/spot capacity books; cargo insurers pricing policies on assumed contracted-carrier risk profiles when actual execution involves spot carriers.

**Estimated cost:**
- Tender rejection premium: average 14.8% price increase when a load moves from contracted carrier to spot market (19)
- Additional cost of carrier rejections: 10–30% above contracted rate depending on market conditions (Acocella & Caplice 2023, JBL) (36)
- On average 70% of FTL contracts go unused in some market conditions — meaning strategic procurement investment is consistently not aligned with actual execution patterns (Acocella & Caplice 2023, JBL) (36)
- Companies spending ~100 hours per week on carrier scorecard data collection, cleaning, and distribution — an indirect labor cost of approximately $130,000–$200,000 annually at senior logistics analyst rates [ASSUMED-10]
- For a shipper with $100M annual freight spend: if 15% of loads move to spot due to routing guide staleness-driven rejections, and spot premium is 14.8%, incremental freight cost is approximately $2.2M annually [ASSUMED-11] — to validate: this figure is modeled, not measured; ask a shipper running SONAR/FreightWaves benchmark data to calculate their actual spot overflow cost
- Routing guide update cadence mismatch: a carrier hitting 94% OTD in Q1 dropping to 71% by Q3 without guide update means the shipper is sending priority volume to an underperforming carrier while potentially bypassing better performers — the opportunity cost of this misallocation is not quantified in public primary research [ASSUMED-11]

**Willingness to pay:** Locus ShipFlex module reports recovering "$288 million in revenue leakage across customers" — a vendor-stated aggregate; specific per-seat WTP not disclosed. Armstrong & Associates (2020 digitization report) identifies carrier scorecard automation as a TMS-adjacent investment area with active demand. [NEEDS-ATTENDED-FETCH]: Gartner Critical Capabilities for TMS 2024 scores carriers on performance management and routing guide automation features that would reveal WTP data.

**Frequency:**
- Routing guide compliance: remained stable at 94% first-tender acceptance in favorable 2024 market conditions, but fell sharply in prior tight-capacity cycles; a 13% rejection rate at peak season is documented (37)
- Carrier performance data quality issues: documented as affecting a substantial proportion of shippers — "erroneous data fueling carrier scorecards causes shippers to struggle to form a narrative around carrier performance...resulting in billions of lost dollars from erroneous retailer chargebacks, suboptimal routing guide decisions" (SPS Commerce, via SupplierWiki) (38)
- Armstrong & Associates (2020): TMS is now "table stakes" for freight brokers — but without performance feedback loops, the TMS enforces a static routing guide rather than a dynamic risk-adjusted allocation (39)

**Evidenced or assumed:**
(36) Acocella & Caplice, "Research on truckload transportation procurement: A review, framework, and future research agenda," Journal of Business Logistics, Volume 2023, DOI 10.1111/jbl.12333 — https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/jbl.12333
(37) Tender rejection rate / routing guide compliance — FreightWaves SONAR — https://gosonar.com/features/outbound-tender-rejection-index; spot market tender rejection rate data — https://blog.gettransport.com/news/spot-market-tender-rejection-rates/
(38) Carrier scorecard data quality — SPS Commerce / SupplierWiki — https://www.spscommerce.com/community/articles/3-scorecard-success-roadblocks
(39) Armstrong & Associates digitization report — https://www.3plogistics.com/armstrong-associates-report-highlights-digitization-the-convergence-of-modern-freight-brokerage-digital-freight-matching-and-automation-in-domestic-transportation-management/
(40) Locus carrier management — routing guide staleness claim — https://locus.sh/blogs/carrier-management/
(41) Triumph carrier scorecarding — 14.8% rejection premium — https://triumph.io/blog/shipper/carrier-scorecarding-benefits-challenges-and-solutions/
(42) C.H. Robinson freight market update September 2024 — routing compliance data — https://www.chrobinson.com/en-us/resources/insights-and-advisories/north-america-freight-insights/september-2024-freight-market-updates/
(43) Keelvar transport tendering guide — https://www.keelvar.com/blog/transport-tendering
(44) FreightWaves tender rejection prediction analysis — https://www.freightwaves.com/news/how-tender-rejections-predict-your-next-rate

**Root Causes**

- **RC1: Carrier performance data is generated in operational systems (ELD, TMS execution logs, claim databases) and consumed in procurement systems (routing guides, sourcing tools), but no standard data exchange protocol connects these two layers in real time** — A shipper's TMS dispatch module records carrier OTD performance for every load. Its procurement module (or separate sourcing tool) governs the routing guide that determines which carrier receives the next tender. These two modules share a data environment within the same TMS vendor's suite but are updated on different cadences — procurement guides are updated quarterly or annually at RFP events, while performance data accumulates daily. No TMS vendor has built a rule that automatically demotes a carrier's routing guide position when its rolling 90-day OTD falls below a threshold. The structural cause is that routing guides were conceived as annual procurement outputs, not as continuously updating risk-allocation instruments. (Acocella & Caplice JBL 2023: https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/jbl.12333; locus.sh: https://locus.sh/blogs/carrier-management/)

- **RC2: The academic freight procurement optimization literature treats strategic contracting and execution-stage dispatch as independent problems, creating a structural validation gap for integrated risk-routing models** — Acocella & Caplice (JBL 2023) explicitly identify that "essentially all strategic buy literature assumes flawless execution" — meaning no academic freight procurement model includes carrier risk signals (safety score degradation, insurance lapse, recent fraud flag) as inputs to the routing guide optimization. This is not a mere academic gap: TMS vendors draw on academic optimization frameworks to develop their routing guide and carrier-allocation engines. If the academic models do not include risk inputs, the commercial implementations will not either, unless vendors independently invest in adding them. The structural cause is a feedback loop: practitioners do not see risk-integrated routing guides as standard, researchers do not study them, vendors do not build them. (Acocella & Caplice: https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/jbl.12333)

- **RC3: Annual freight RFP processes lock carrier allocation for 12 months, making routing guide adjustments structurally difficult even when performance data clearly warrants them** — Most enterprise shippers run annual freight RFP events that award lane volume to carriers for a 12-month period. Mid-year routing guide adjustments require renegotiating rates with the underperforming carrier, sourcing a replacement, and onboarding the new carrier's EDI connection — a process that takes 4–8 weeks minimum for EDI onboarding alone. The commercial and operational cost of mid-cycle reallocation is high enough that shippers tolerate underperforming carriers rather than trigger a reallocation event. The structural cause is that the 12-month contracting cycle was designed to give carriers revenue certainty and shippers rate stability — but this stability comes at the cost of risk responsiveness. [ASSUMED-11]: No primary survey has quantified the proportion of shippers who identify a carrier as underperforming but do not update their routing guide mid-cycle due to reallocation cost. — to validate: Ask five shipper transportation directors about their last mid-cycle carrier reallocation and what triggered it.

- **RC4: Carrier vetting is performed at onboarding and then treated as a persistent approval that does not decay with compliance changes** — The standard carrier onboarding process (RMIS, Highway, SaferWatch, Carrier411) performs a compliance check at point-in-time and flags ongoing changes. However, the routing guide in the TMS typically reflects the carrier's status at onboarding or last annual review — not their real-time compliance state. A carrier that passes onboarding with a Satisfactory FMCSA rating and then receives a Conditional rating three months later remains on the approved-carrier list and continues receiving auto-tender volume until a human dispatcher manually notices the change. The structural cause is that real-time compliance monitoring feeds exist (Carrier411 alerts, Highway contact-change monitoring) but are not natively connected to routing guide priority rules in TMS dispatch engines. (Carrier411 monitoring: https://www.carrier411.com/carrier-monitoring-service.cfm; locus.sh: https://locus.sh/blogs/carrier-management/)

**SEA implication:** The performance-feedback-to-routing-guide disconnect is structural in Western TMS environments but is essentially total in SEA, where: (a) no FMCSA-equivalent registry provides real-time safety rating data, (b) digital freight platforms use reputation scores rather than regulatory compliance data, and (c) routing guides as a formal construct exist only in the largest multinational shippers' operations. For SEA mid-market shippers, carrier allocation is entirely relationship-driven with no systematic performance feedback loop — making the pain more severe and more invisible simultaneously.

---

### Pain point 2.5

**Description:** Shippers and 3PLs operating in multi-modal freight environments (combining ocean, air, and truck) face a structural orchestration fragmentation: each mode is typically managed in a separate system or TMS module with incompatible data structures, creating visibility gaps and risk-context loss at every mode handoff. A shipper managing ocean-to-truck intermodal freight may have an ocean TMS (Kuehne+Nagel's GENIUS, Maersk's Twill, or a freight forwarder portal), a separate domestic TMS (SAP TM, Oracle OTM), and a visibility platform (project44, FourKites) — none of which share a unified risk profile for the shipment as it transitions between modes. The IoT sensor that was monitoring temperature on the ocean leg is not recognized by the truck carrier's dispatch system. The cargo value and special handling requirements documented in the ocean TMS are not transmitted to the domestic TMS auto-tender. Costly "blind handoffs" — the transitions where goods move between manufacturers, warehouses, and delivery partners without unified data — account for **$65B–$95B annually (13–19% of US logistics costs)** per McKinsey & Company's January 2024 analysis (the trade-press citation that omitted the lower bound has been replaced with the direct McKinsey URL).

**Who bears it:** Multinational shipper transportation managers managing ocean-air-truck supply chains; 3PL operations directors orchestrating multi-modal logistics on behalf of shippers; freight forwarders managing end-to-end shipments across modes; cargo insurers pricing policies for multi-modal shipments where risk profiles change by mode.

**Estimated cost:**
- "Blind handoff" logistics cost estimate: **$65B–$95B per year (13–19% of US logistics costs)** per McKinsey (Jan 2024) — B2B accounts for $45B–$66B/yr; B2C $18B–$28B/yr; carriers bear 6–13% of their overall revenue from this waste. This figure covers all supply-chain handoff inefficiencies; the multi-modal TMS contribution is a subset. **Deep-research outcome: found-stronger-evidence; trade-press citation replaced with McKinsey primary URL; the original $95B / 19% point estimates were the upper bound of a published range.** (45)
- Freight invoice errors from multi-modal rate reconciliation: 3–7% of freight spend; multi-modal freight generates higher error rates because rates, surcharges, and accessorials differ by mode and are often entered manually at transition points (18)
- When a temperature-sensitive shipment loses cold-chain monitoring at mode handoff and damage occurs, cargo loss exposure is the full replacement value of the load — for pharmaceuticals, this can be $500K–$5M per incident [ASSUMED-13]. CargoNet Q3 2024 identifies pharmaceutical, food, and electronics as the highest-value cargo theft/damage targets (29)
- ERP/TMS integration cost for adding a new modal connection: €50,000–€150,000 per modal integration (transportmanagement.org) (46)

**Willingness to pay:** project44's Intelligent TMS launch (2025) explicitly targets multi-modal orchestration fragmentation as its primary value proposition, indicating commercial demand for unified multi-modal visibility. Transporeon (acquired by Trimble 2023 for ~€1.88B) built its growth on European road-to-rail multi-modal orchestration. Specific per-seat ACV for multi-modal TMS not publicly disclosed.

**Frequency:**
- "Many companies still manage modes in silos — one system for truckload, spreadsheets for everything else" (Princeton TMX analysis) (47)
- Infrastructure fragmentation in multi-modal TMS "demands sophisticated data harmonization across incompatible systems" — identified as a primary adoption barrier by multiple TMS analyst reviews in 2024–2025 (48)
- SEA-specific: Indonesia's island geography creates mandatory multi-modal freight (road-to-sea-to-road) for nearly all inter-island shipments, making multi-modal orchestration a baseline requirement, not an advanced capability — yet the TMS tools to manage this are not deployed at scale (49)

**Evidenced or assumed:**
(45) McKinsey & Company, "Digitizing mid- and last-mile logistics handovers to reduce waste," January 5, 2024 — https://www.mckinsey.com/industries/logistics/our-insights/digitizing-mid-and-last-mile-logistics-handovers-to-reduce-waste — Primary consultancy-authored source. Range: $65B–$95B annually, 13–19% of US logistics costs; B2B $45B–$66B; B2C $18B–$28B; carriers absorb 6–13% of revenue. Replaces prior trade-press citation (transportandlogisticsme.com) which selectively cited the upper bound only.
(46) TMS modal integration cost €50K–€150K — transportmanagement.org European shipper multimodal TMS analysis — https://www.transportmanagement.org/the-european-shippers-multimodal-tms-resilience-strategy-how-to-build-corridor-adaptive-transport-management-that-thrives-despite-ten-t-delays-while-securing-vendor-resilient-platforms-/
(47) Princeton TMX — modes managed in silos — https://www.princetontmx.com/blogs/multimodal-tms-software-for-industrial-shippers
(48) TMS modal fragmentation — CTSI-Global analysis — https://ctsi-global.com/2024/simplify-multi-modal-transportation-using-an-adaptable-tms/; project44 Intelligent TMS — https://www.project44.com/press-releases/project44-unveils-intelligent-tms-a-new-era-of-agile-ai-driven-freight-management-for-modern-supply-chains
(49) Indonesia island multi-modal requirement — Vynn Capital Indonesia Logistics Report 2024 — vynncapital.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/11/Indonesia-Logistics-Report.pdf; Inbound Logistics SEA logistics puzzle — https://www.inboundlogistics.com/articles/solving-the-southeast-asia-logistics-puzzle/
(50) Supply Chain Brain — TMS must support air and ocean — https://www.supplychainbrain.com/articles/18574-your-tms-must-support-air-cargo-and-ocean-transportation-to-be-truly-multimodal
(51) CXTMS multimodal rate management 2026 — https://cxtms.com/blog/multimodal-rate-management-platforms-unified-pricing-freight-procurement-2026
(52) project44 Intelligent TMS press release (2025) — https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/project44-unveils-intelligent-tms-a-new-era-of-agile-ai-driven-freight-management-for-modern-supply-chains-302532754.html
(53) Pando multimodal TMS streaming analysis — https://pando.ai/blogs/streamline-multimodal-shipping-with-a-tms

**Root Causes**

- **RC1: Each freight mode developed its own proprietary data standards and booking platforms before multi-modal orchestration was commercially prioritized** — Ocean shipping runs on EDI 315/322/350 and carrier-specific portals (Maersk, MSC, CMA-CGM each have separate APIs); air cargo uses IATA standards (Cargo XML, CIMP); domestic trucking uses EDI 204/990/214 or proprietary load-board APIs. These standards evolved independently and are not designed to carry a unified shipment-level risk profile across mode transitions. A TMS integrating ocean and truck freight must transform data between at least two incompatible standards at the handoff, and the transformation logic typically drops fields that are not represented in both standards — including cargo value, special handling codes, and sensor device identifiers. The structural cause is that mode-specific booking platforms predate the commercial demand for multi-modal orchestration, and no standards body has developed a mode-agnostic freight data envelope. (CTSI-Global multimodal TMS: https://ctsi-global.com/2024/simplify-multi-modal-transportation-using-an-adaptable-tms/; CXTMS: https://cxtms.com/blog/multimodal-rate-management-platforms-unified-pricing-freight-procurement-2026)

- **RC2: IoT cargo monitoring devices are bound to a specific carrier or leg at deployment, with no cross-carrier identifier that survives mode handoffs** — A temperature logger activated at a cold-chain warehouse and linked to an ocean carrier's Bill of Lading is not recognized by the domestic truck carrier's dispatch system at the port of discharge. The sensor device ID is not transmitted in the EDI handoff documents that transfer custody. WH3 from input hypotheses — that IoT sensors are point-deployed rather than journey-deployed — is confirmed as structurally real at the TMS/orchestration layer: TMS systems do not carry IoT sensor identifiers in their carrier tender documents, meaning there is no systematic mechanism for a new carrier to inherit monitoring responsibility for a sensor that is already active on the cargo. [ASSUMED-14]: No primary survey has quantified what percentage of multi-modal cold-chain shipments experience a monitoring gap at mode handoff. — to validate: Interview a cold-chain cargo manager at a pharmaceutical distributor about their temperature monitoring continuity protocol at ocean-to-truck handoff.

- **RC3: Freight forwarders managing multi-modal shipments operate their own TMS that does not expose sub-execution data to the shipper's TMS, creating a parallel to the 3PL sub-carrier visibility gap** — When a shipper delegates multi-modal orchestration to a freight forwarder, the forwarder books each mode through their own systems (Cargowise, WiseTech, Flexport) and presents the shipper with a single Bill of Lading and tracking reference. The shipper's TMS sees the shipment as a single forwarding event, not a sequence of mode-specific carrier bookings with individual risk profiles. The structural cause mirrors Pain point 2.3 (3PL sub-carrier visibility) but at modal scale: forwarders have the same commercial incentive to protect their carrier network from direct shipper access, and no technical standard requires forwarders to expose mode-level carrier identity to shipper TMS APIs. (Alpega TMS/carrier network synergies: https://www.alpegagroup.com/en-en/community/blog/unlocking-logistics-potential-synergies-between-tendering-transportation-management-systems-and-open-carrier-networks/)

**SEA implication:** Multi-modal fragmentation is acutely painful in SEA because the geography mandates multi-modal freight (Indonesia: road + sea for inter-island; Thailand/Vietnam: road + rail cross-border; Singapore: air + sea + truck for hub-and-spoke). Yet TMS penetration among SEA shippers is low, and the digital freight platforms that have scaled (Kargo, Deliveree, Haulio) are single-mode road freight specialists. Multi-modal orchestration in SEA is handled primarily by freight forwarders using proprietary tools, meaning the shipper's risk visibility at mode handoff is essentially zero. This is structurally identical to the visibility gap described in Pain point 2.3 but compounded by mode transition.

---

### New pain points (not in original hypotheses)

#### Pain point 2.6 (surfaced through research — beyond WH1/WH2/WH3)

**Description:** Enterprise shippers and 3PLs that have implemented TMS and deployed carrier compliance monitoring (RMIS, Highway, Carrier411) face a data-latency gap between when a carrier's risk profile changes (insurance lapse, FMCSA rating downgrade, ownership transfer, contact information replacement) and when that change propagates to the TMS routing guide and dispatch queue. Carrier411 monitoring alerts are event-triggered, but the TMS routing guide update that demotes the carrier requires a human dispatcher to review the alert, manually update the carrier record, and re-sequence the routing guide — a workflow that takes hours to days, during which the compromised carrier may receive and execute additional auto-tendered loads. Highway detected 109,672 FMCSA contact changes in 2024 as potential fraud signals; Descartes MacroPoint FraudGuard 2.0 (July 2025) explicitly adds pre-tender and pre-pickup checks as a new product capability — confirming that the between-alert-and-dispatch-update gap is a commercially recognized and unsolved problem in the TMS ecosystem.

**Who bears it:** Freight brokers and 3PLs whose automated TMS dispatch routes loads to carriers whose compliance status has degraded since their last routing guide update; shippers whose cargo moves on carriers with lapsed insurance or downgraded FMCSA ratings before the TMS alert is acted upon.

**Estimated cost:**
- A single cargo load dispatched to a carrier with lapsed insurance where cargo is damaged or stolen: full replacement value of cargo, uninsured. For high-value freight (electronics, pharmaceuticals, automotive parts), exposure ranges from $50K to $5M per incident [ASSUMED-15]
- Insurance lapse-to-detection window: if monitoring alerts are reviewed once daily, a carrier whose insurance lapses at 6pm Monday may receive auto-tendered loads until the alert is reviewed Tuesday morning — a 12+ hour window during which multiple loads may have been dispatched [ASSUMED-15]
- Descartes MacroPoint FraudGuard 2.0 adds pre-tender check as a net-new capability (July 2025), implying the pre-tender check capability did not exist before, and therefore the dispatch-lag exposure was unaddressed in most TMS workflows before that date

**Willingness to pay:** Carrier411, RMIS, and Highway monitoring subscriptions are actively purchased by freight brokers as a category, confirming WTP for compliance monitoring. Descartes MacroPoint FraudGuard 2.0's commercial launch confirms continued demand for more real-time dispatch-gate controls.

**Frequency:**
- Highway detected 109,672 FMCSA contact changes in 2024 that were potential fraud signals — this is the volume of compliance-state changes occurring annually in the carrier population that require dispatch-gate validation (5)
- FMCSA CSA overhaul approved late 2024 (implementation date TBD) will change how carrier risk scores are computed — creating an additional compliance-state update event that TMS routing guides will need to incorporate (FMCSA CSA overhaul: https://www.foleyservices.com/articles/staying-off-fmcsa-risk-based-priority-lists)

**Evidenced or assumed:**
(5) Highway 2024 Freight Fraud Trends — 109,672 FMCSA contact changes — https://highway.com/posts/2024-freight-fraud-trends
(7) Descartes MacroPoint FraudGuard 2.0 — https://www.descartes.com/resources/news/descartes-macropoint-fraudguard-provides-transportation-industry-next-generation-solution-for-freight-fraud-defense
(54) Carrier411 carrier monitoring service — https://www.carrier411.com/carrier-monitoring-service.cfm
(55) FMCSA CSA overhaul — Foley Services analysis — https://www.foleyservices.com/articles/staying-off-fmcsa-risk-based-priority-lists
(56) Locus carrier management — compliance lag — https://locus.sh/blogs/carrier-management/

**Root Causes**

- **RC1: Compliance monitoring alert systems and TMS dispatch engines are architecturally separate, with no native API connection that automatically updates routing guide priority when a compliance event is detected** — Carrier411, RMIS, and Highway are compliance-data products; SAP TM, Oracle OTM, and MercuryGate are dispatch products. Their integration is point-to-point custom work, not a native feature. When a compliance alert fires (insurance lapse, FMCSA rating change), it triggers a notification workflow (email, dashboard flag) that requires human review before the routing guide is updated. No TMS vendor has built a compliance-event-to-routing-guide-update rule as a default product feature — the closest approach is Descartes MacroPoint FraudGuard 2.0's pre-tender check (July 2025), which is an add-on integration, not a native TMS function.

- **RC2: The carrier compliance monitoring market evolved as a separate product category from TMS dispatch, creating a product ecosystem where the two categories share no common data standard for compliance-event propagation** — RMIS, Carrier411, and Highway developed as carrier onboarding and monitoring tools before TMS dispatch automation became the industry norm. Their data outputs (alert feeds, compliance reports) are not formatted to be consumed by TMS routing guide engines. Integrating them requires custom ETL work that sits outside both vendors' standard product scope. The structural cause is that two product categories with complementary functions developed independently, creating an integration gap that each buyer must solve individually. [ASSUMED-16]: The proportion of TMS-deploying freight brokers and shippers that have built a live compliance-alert-to-routing-guide integration (vs. relying on human-reviewed alerts) is not publicly surveyed. — to validate: Ask five enterprise TMS administrators whether their TMS auto-updates carrier routing guide positions based on real-time compliance alerts.

---

### Hypothesis assessment for Stage 2

**WH1 (carrier vetting data not feeding into tendering): CONFIRMED AND STRENGTHENED.** The Acocella/Caplice (JBL 2023) academic review confirms the structural procurement-execution gap. Pain points 2.1, 2.4, and 2.6 all document this disconnect from different angles. The new finding is that the gap operates at three layers simultaneously: (a) fraud/identity at dispatch time (PP 2.1), (b) performance data not updating routing guides (PP 2.4), and (c) compliance state changes not propagating to dispatch gates (PP 2.6).

**WH2 (visibility breaks at handoff — TMS sees tender to 3PL but not what 3PL does after): CONFIRMED BUT PARTIALLY MODIFIED.** The pain is real (PP 2.3) but is partially mitigated in enterprise shipper contexts through carrier-approval clauses in 3PL MSAs. The residual pain is concentrated in mid-market shippers without contracting sophistication and in SEA markets where sub-carrier chains are informal. The hypothesis was too universal; the correct framing is: "visibility breaks at 3PL handoff for shippers without formal subcontractor governance frameworks."

**WH3 (IoT sensors point-deployed not journey-deployed): CONFIRMED AT THE TMS/ORCHESTRATION LAYER.** TMS carrier tender documents do not carry IoT sensor device identifiers; there is no standard for cross-carrier sensor identity transfer. The pain is real and documented in PP 2.5. However, this pain is secondary to the broader multi-modal orchestration fragmentation — WH3 is one component of a larger mode-handoff data loss problem.

**One hypothesis partially rejected:** The prior draft implied that mid-market TMS implementation failure (PP 2.2) was primarily a risk-management failing. Research shows it is primarily a technology adoption and ROI failing — risk management is the *consequence* of failure, not the *cause*. The framing has been revised accordingly.

---

### Assumptions tagged in this stage

[ASSUMED-1]: The $500M–$700M industry-wide freight fraud figure originates from a TriumphPay/Highway commercial press release (April 2023) and has been re-cited without independent methodological verification. No FBI IC3, FMCSA, Verisk CargoNet, Marsh, or Allianz primary dataset independently corroborates this figure. Use as directional only. — to validate: Request methodology disclosure from TriumphPay actuarial team or Marsh cargo practice.

[ASSUMED-2]: No primary survey has quantified the fraud-rate differential between spot and contracted carrier pools within a single TMS. The structural reasoning (spot pools include higher-risk carriers) is supported by CargoNet strategic theft data and practitioner commentary but not by a carrier-type-segmented fraud dataset. — to validate: Ask a freight broker operating a hybrid contracted/spot TMS to pull fraud incident data by carrier pool type for the last 12 months.

[ASSUMED-3]: SEA digital freight platforms have not published fraud incident statistics. The structural analysis rests on market fragmentation data (Indonesia logistics cost 14.29% of GDP per WCO 2024; micro-fleet dominance) and inference from US digital freight fraud analog. — to validate: Interview a compliance officer at Kargo Technologies or Haulio about their carrier identity verification process at dispatch.

[ASSUMED-4]: Mid-market TMS total first-year cost of $300K–$720K is cited in Nuvocargo and transportmanagement.org analyses but these are vendor-adjacent sources. No independent SI cost disclosure is publicly available. — to validate: Ask a Tier-1 SI (Accenture Supply Chain, Capgemini) what they budget for a mid-tier manufacturer's cloud TMS initial go-live.

[ASSUMED-5]: Enterprise SAP TM / Oracle OTM implementation costs of $2M–$6M year-1 professional services are directionally consistent with industry benchmarks but Gartner Critical Capabilities for TMS and Forrester TEI reports are paywalled. — [NEEDS-ATTENDED-FETCH]: Gartner Critical Capabilities for TMS 2024; Forrester TEI for SAP TM.

[ASSUMED-6]: Primary survey data on SEA mid-market TMS adoption rates does not exist in publicly available form. The SEA analysis rests on market fragmentation reports and inference from global adoption patterns. — to validate: Survey transportation managers at 20 mid-market manufacturers in Indonesia and Thailand about their current carrier management process.

[ASSUMED-7]: Mid-size 3PL claims processing cost of $250K–$750K annually (50 claims × $5K–$15K) is constructed from general claims management cost benchmarks; no primary survey of 3PL claims processing cost by volume exists. — to validate: Interview claims managers at three mid-size 3PLs about their annual cargo claim volume and average cost per claim to resolve.

[ASSUMED-8]: The extent to which 3PLs actively resist sub-carrier transparency (commercial disincentive) vs. simply lacking the technical capability to provide it has not been surveyed publicly. — to validate: Interview 3PL operations directors at three mid-size 3PLs about whether sub-carrier visibility is a client request they receive, and how they respond.

[ASSUMED-9]: The proportion of SEA freight moved through informal sub-carrier chains with no formal compliance tracking is not quantified in public primary research. — to validate: Interview a cargo insurance underwriter specializing in SEA trucking about how they price policies where sub-carrier chains are informal.

[ASSUMED-10]: Annual labor cost of carrier scorecard data management (100 hours/week at senior analyst rates) is constructed from the SPS Commerce claim of "nearly 100 hours a week" and standard US logistics analyst salary ranges. The 100-hour figure is from a single case study, not an industry average. — to validate: Survey five shipper transportation teams about their weekly hours spent on carrier performance data collection and cleaning.

[ASSUMED-11]: Incremental spot freight cost from routing guide staleness ($2.2M annually for a $100M freight spend shipper) is modeled from the 14.8% spot premium and an assumed 15% spot overflow rate due to routing guide staleness. Neither the overflow rate nor the staleness attribution are from primary research. — to validate: Ask a shipper running SONAR/FreightWaves benchmark data to calculate their actual spot overflow attributable to routing guide staleness vs. market-driven rejections.

[ASSUMED-12 RESOLVED]: Primary source located — McKinsey & Company, "Digitizing mid- and last-mile logistics handovers to reduce waste," January 2024. Direct URL confirmed; range $65B–$95B (13–19% of US logistics costs). Trade-press citation replaced.

[ASSUMED-13]: Pharmaceutical cargo loss exposure of $500K–$5M per temperature excursion incident is estimated from general high-value cargo ranges. CargoNet does not break out pharmaceutical loss by cause (theft vs. temperature excursion). — to validate: Interview a cargo insurer specializing in pharmaceutical cold-chain about their average per-incident loss for temperature excursion claims.

[ASSUMED-14]: No primary survey has quantified the percentage of multi-modal cold-chain shipments that experience a monitoring gap at mode handoff. This is inferred from the structural absence of cross-carrier IoT sensor identifier standards in TMS tender documents. — to validate: Interview a cold-chain cargo manager at a pharmaceutical distributor about their temperature monitoring continuity protocol at ocean-to-truck handoff.

[ASSUMED-15]: The 12+ hour compliance-state-change-to-routing-guide-update window is constructed from operational logic (daily alert review cycles, human-in-loop update process) rather than measured data. The per-incident cargo exposure of $50K–$5M is estimated from general high-value cargo ranges. — to validate: Ask five enterprise freight broker TMS administrators about their compliance alert review cadence and TMS update process.

[ASSUMED-16]: The proportion of TMS-deploying freight brokers and shippers that have built a live compliance-alert-to-routing-guide integration (vs. relying on human-reviewed alerts) is not publicly surveyed. — to validate: Ask five enterprise TMS administrators whether their TMS auto-updates carrier routing guide positions based on real-time compliance alerts.

# Stage 3: Fleet & Driver Management / Telematics — Pain Points
## Worldwide Solution-Landscape Scan — Logistics Risk Management
*Research date: 2026-05-17 | Pass 1 draft (improved) | Supersedes 2026-05-14 version*

---

## Stage 3: Fleet & Driver Management / Telematics

**Actors:** Carrier ops managers, driver safety leads, fleet maintenance directors using fleet/driver/telematics solutions; shippers and 3PLs who depend on carrier-side telematics data they do not own; commercial auto and cargo insurers who cannot access the behavioral data they need to price risk accurately.

**Scope note:** Stage 3 covers the fleet telematics layer — what the carrier owns, what it does not share, and the structural consequences of that non-sharing. Stage 4 covers cargo IoT (the shipment-level sensor layer). The Stage 3–Stage 4 interface is the carrier's own telematics platform (ELD + dashcam + GPS), which is the data origin for both. GPS jamming as a countermeasure is flagged in PP 3.1 (as a gap in the subcontracted-leg dark zone) and developed at Stage 4 depth there.

---

### Pain point 3.1

**Description:** When a shipper hires a 3PL that subcontracts to a small or mid-size carrier, the continuous telematics record generated during that carrier's leg of the journey is structurally owned — and contractually gatekept — by the carrier as the sole platform subscriber. Samsara's platform terms explicitly state that Samsara will not share customer data without customer consent, and integration partners cannot redistribute customer data to third parties. The 3PL's real-time transportation visibility platform (project44, FourKites, Shippeo) cannot subscribe to that telematics feed without the carrier actively authorizing an API token — a multi-step process almost never completed for short-term or spot-contracted subcarriers. The result is a structural dark zone at the carrier handoff layer: cargo journeys lose continuous telematics coverage at precisely the point where cargo theft, damage, and delay risk is highest. When an incident occurs on a subcontracted leg with no telematics record, incident response — by the carrier, the 3PL, the insurer — becomes manual, slow, and contested. This is not a technology failure; it is a data-governance design that correctly protects carrier interests while creating a gap that no downstream party can resolve unilaterally.

**Who bears it:** Shippers (cargo owners) who lose visibility at the subcontracted-leg handoff; 3PLs whose service-level commitments to shippers include real-time visibility they cannot guarantee across the carrier tier; cargo insurers who cannot validate transit compliance on subcontracted freight; end customers receiving unexplained delays and missing ETAs.

**Estimated cost:**
- Global in-transit cargo theft: $6.6 billion in North America alone in 2025, up 18% year-over-year; 41% of incidents occur during transit, 76% involve trucks — telematics dark zones on subcontracted legs structurally amplify exposure during the highest-risk moments (1, 2)
- Average cargo theft value per incident (North America 2024): $202,364 per event; incidents on subcontracted legs with no telematics audit trail impede recovery and make insurance subrogation harder (3)
- Shipper spend to close the visibility gap: RTTVP market grew to $6.3 billion globally in 2024; enterprise shippers spend $200K–$500K+ per year on visibility platforms, yet the subcontracted-leg dark zone persists even after purchase — demonstrating that shippers are paying a large and recurring amount to fix a problem that is only partially fixable without carrier telematics authorization (4)
- Incident response cost premium on non-telematics legs: when a cargo incident occurs on a subcontracted leg with no telematics record, claims investigation requires manual reconstruction (driver statements, fuel receipt cross-referencing, toll records), adding weeks to settlement timelines and creating liability disputes that increase settlement values [ASSUMED-1]: This cost premium is estimated at 20–40% of standard claims-handling cost per incident — to validate: request claims investigation time data from Marsh, Chubb, or TT Club for cargo claims where no ELD/GPS trail was available vs. claims with a full telematics record

**Willingness to pay:** project44 processes visibility data for 1 billion+ shipments annually across 1,300+ enterprise shippers and has 150,000+ carriers in its network (80,000 immediately activatable via Connection Accelerator). Shippeo's spot-carrier tracking product explicitly exists to address the case where "subcontractors' vehicles aren't already connected" — these are workarounds paying vendors to partially close the gap, not solutions to the underlying data-ownership structure. (5, 6)

**Frequency:**
- SEA: 82.5% of commercial fleet vehicles have no fleet management system (Berg Insight, 2025 edition — 17.5% penetration in 2024, rising to 27.1% by 2029); the fraction that has FMS largely runs basic trackers with no API layer (7)
- US/EU: ELD mandate (FMCSA, full compliance December 2019) means near-universal ELD coverage for CDL interstate carriers, but ELD data is owned by the carrier and requires active authorization to flow to third parties — data exists but does not flow by default (8)
- project44's "preferred carrier" list covers 3,580 carriers across 70+ countries — less than 2% of its 150,000+ carrier network — indicating deep telematics integration is the exception even among premium network participants (5)
- Spot market tender rejection rates exceeded 13% at 2025 peak season, forcing loads into spot carrier pools where telematics authorization rates are structurally lower than for contracted carriers (see Stage 2, PP 2.1)

**Evidenced or assumed:**
(1) TT Club / BSI Cargo Theft Report 2025 — $6.6B North America, +18% YoY — https://www.insurancebusinessmag.com/us/news/breaking-news/global-cargo-theft-hits-new-highs-as-criminal-networks-evolve--tt-club-572468.aspx
(2) TT Club / BSI 2024 — 41% during transit, 76% trucks — https://www.ttclub.com/news-and-resources/news/article/cargo-theft-report-reveals-rapidly-shifting-criminal-tactics-and-emerging-targets/
(3) Verisk CargoNet 2024 — 3,625 incidents, $454.9M, avg $202,364/incident — https://www.verisk.com/company/newsroom/cargo-theft-surges-to-record-levels-in-2024-verisk-cargonet-analysis-reveals/
(4) RTTVP market $6.3B in 2024 — ResearchAndMarkets via BusinessWire — https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20250820985707/en/Real-Time-Transportation-Visibility-Platforms-Report-2025-Project44-FourKites-and-Shippeo-Lead-the-Charge-in-Visibility-Platforms---ResearchAndMarkets.com
(5) project44 Connection Accelerator — 1B+ shipments, 1,300+ enterprise shippers, 150,000+ carriers — https://www.project44.com/press-releases/project44-doubles-down-on-connectivity-infrastructure-with-launch-of-connection-center/
(6) Shippeo spot-carrier tracking — "If the subcontractor's vehicle isn't already connected ... there can be no real-time tracking" — https://www.shippeo.com/blog/tracking-spot-carrier-shipments-the-visibility-gap-that-doesnt-have-to-be
(7) Berg Insight Fleet Management in Southeast Asia 2nd Edition, 2025 — https://iotbusinessnews.com/2026/02/26/the-installed-base-of-fleet-management-systems-in-southeast-asia-to-reach-6-4-million-units-by-2029/
(8) FMCSA ELD mandate — full compliance December 2019 — https://www.fmcsa.dot.gov/hours-service/elds/general-information-about-eld-rule
(9) Samsara platform terms — data-sharing requires express customer consent — https://www.samsara.com/legal/platform-terms-of-service
(10) project44 preferred carriers — 3,580 carriers, <2% of network — https://www.project44.com/blog/2024-preferred-carrier-awards/
(11) Shippeo Driver App / MacroPoint app-less tracking as workaround products — https://macropoint.com/news/rttvp/
(12) North Penn Now — data silos in multimodal transport — https://northpennnow.com/news/2026/jan/02/breaking-data-silos-connectivity-challenges-in-multimodal-transportation/

**Root Causes**

- **RC1: Telematics platforms are architected for single-organization fleet management, with no native multi-party data-sharing model.** Samsara, Motive, and equivalent platforms sell to the fleet operator (the carrier) as their sole contracting party. Every data-access control protects the fleet operator's data from unauthorized third-party access — a correct and deliberate design for its intended use case. The platform has no commercial relationship with the 3PL or shipper on any individual shipment, and therefore no mechanism to extend data rights to them without explicit carrier action. Samsara's integration partner terms state: "Integration Partner shall not sell, license, sublicense, or otherwise transfer or disclose any Customer Data obtained through Samsara's Products, API or platform to any third party without express customer consent." The multi-party freight relationship (shipper → 3PL → carrier → subcarrier) was not the design context for any of these platforms. (Samsara Master Terms: https://www.samsara.com/legal/platform-terms-of-service; Samsara–project44 integration requiring carrier-initiated API token: https://kb.samsara.com/hc/en-us/articles/360022557031-Integrate-with-project44)

- **RC2: No industry standard or freight contract clause requires carriers to authorize telematics data sharing with tendering parties.** The TMC (Technology & Maintenance Council) S.12 working group has produced Recommended Practice RP 1226 on Open Telematics API (OTAPI) and message standardization, but adoption is voluntary and the standard does not address multi-party data access rights across freight contracts. No ATA or TIA model freight tender clause currently requires the contracted carrier — let alone the subcarrier — to authorize telematics data sharing with the shipper or 3PL. Every subcontract is negotiated on terms prioritizing rate, transit time, and capacity, not telematics authorization. (TMC RP 1226: https://www.ttnews.com/articles/data-interchangeinteroperability-challenges-carriers-take-action)

- **RC3: Small carriers have a rational commercial incentive not to authorize third-party data access, and no countervailing commercial pressure to do so.** A small carrier sharing telematics data with a 3PL or shipper exposes its routing patterns, driver schedules, idle time, and fuel efficiency — commercially sensitive information that could be used to pressure rates or switch carriers on future tenders. The authorization is asymmetric: the carrier bears all the exposure, the upside is nil (the carrier already knows where its trucks are), and no freight contract currently provides a direct commercial incentive (rate premium, preferred-carrier status, faster payment) in exchange for telematics authorization on a subcontracted leg. [ASSUMED-2]: The hypothesis that small carriers systematically decline or never respond to telematics authorization requests is well-supported by structural logic and practitioner commentary but has not been measured by a published survey — to validate: ask 20 small carriers in Indonesia and Vietnam whether a 3PL has ever requested telematics API access, and what their response was.

- **RC4: Proliferation of incompatible telematics platforms makes cross-party data normalization technically and economically impractical at the subcarrier tier.** project44 lists integrations with 1,000+ telematics and ELD providers; Samsara alone covers 350+ app marketplace partners. But the long tail of regional providers used by small SEA carriers — TransTRACK (Indonesia), GPSiam (Thailand), Katsana (Malaysia), plus dozens of Jimi IoT white-label deployments — do not have pre-built integrations with enterprise RTTVPs. Even if a small carrier authorized data sharing, the receiving RTTVP would need to build or maintain an integration with that carrier's specific telematics provider. The economic case for building integrations with providers whose aggregate fleet is fewer than 50,000 vehicles is weak; enterprise RTTVPs optimize integration investment toward carriers generating the highest shipment volumes. (Berg Insight SEA 2025: https://iotbusinessnews.com/2026/02/26/the-installed-base-of-fleet-management-systems-in-southeast-asia-to-reach-6-4-million-units-by-2029/)

---

### Pain point 3.2

**Description:** Commercial auto and cargo insurers underwrite carrier risk based on declared cargo values, historical loss records, DOT inspection scores, and CSA safety measurements — not the granular, continuous, per-driver behavioral stream captured by video telematics platforms. This gap is quantifiable: 88% of fleets now run telematics programs, but only 30% share that data with their commercial auto insurer (SambaSafety, 2025; n=152 fleet professionals, 180 insurance brokers, 70 carriers). A separate finding from the same survey: 74% of fleets that do not share data say it is because they were never asked. The consequence is a market where 14 consecutive years of commercial auto underwriting losses — with a 2024 combined ratio of 113 on the liability line and estimated under-reserving of $4–$5 billion — persist while the behavioral data most predictive of future losses sits inside fleet management platforms unused by insurers. Telematics-native underwriters (Nirvana, HDVI) are demonstrating that behavioral underwriting is actuarially viable at scale, but they serve a niche of safety-conscious early adopters; the mainstream of commercial auto insurance has not restructured around the data.

**Who bears it:** Cargo insurers and commercial auto insurers who continue to missprice risk on fleets with strong behavioral profiles vs. weak ones; safety-conscious fleet operators who cross-subsidize peers with poor driver behavior through blended class-wide pricing (adverse selection); shippers whose cargo moves on unverified-behavior carrier tiers; the broader commercial auto market, which has posted $10+ billion in net underwriting losses over the past two years.

**Estimated cost:**
- Commercial auto **liability sub-line** posted a 2024 combined ratio of **113** (vs. blended segment combined ratio of ~107, because the physical-damage sub-line ran at 88.6 and pulled the blended figure down); cumulative net underwriting losses exceed **$10 billion** over 2023–2024 ($4.9B in 2024, ~$5B in 2023); AM Best estimates the segment is under-reserved by $4–$5 billion — AM Best Market Segment Report "US Commercial Auto Insurance Segment Stuck in Reverse," September 2025 (1) [Deep-researched 2026-05-18: 113 figure confirmed for liability sub-line; segment overall is ~107; both correct for their scope.]
- Insurance premiums hit **$0.102 per vehicle mile** in 2024 — a record high, up 3.0% from $0.099/mile in 2023 — representing 4.5% of total operating costs ($2.260/mile) for a US carrier; this $0.102 metric covers auto liability + cargo coverage and excludes physical damage. ATRI's 2025 Operational Costs of Trucking report identifies auto liability premiums as having risen **36% per mile over the past 8 years** (ATRI Nov 2025 press release), even as truck crashes declined (2) [Deep-researched 2026-05-18: $0.102 confirmed; 8.7¢ summaries referenced earlier-year ATRI editions.]
- Trucking-specific nuclear verdict exposure: Marathon Strategies documents 8 trucking-only nuclear verdicts in 2024 totaling ~$790.5M; trucking + automotive combined verdicts reached 15 cases totaling $4.1B (3) [Note: Marathon Strategies' broader 135-verdict cross-industry total of $31.3B covers all corporate defendants — trucking is a subset, not the aggregate]
- Premium asymmetry: telematics-insured fleets demonstrably earn 10–40% lower premiums (Northland/Travelers: 10% discount for Samsara dashcam; Motive–GEICO: up to 10% savings); for a 100-truck fleet running 80,000 miles/truck/year at $0.102/mile, total annual insurance = $816K; a 15% unrealized behavioral discount = $122K/year in excess premium paid by safe fleets that do not share data (4, 5)
- Nirvana Insurance, the most advanced telematics-native underwriter, reported "premiums under management worth more than $100 million" as of its Series C in March 2025 at an $830M valuation — confirming institutional capital believes behavioral underwriting is viable, but also confirming the mainstream market has not yet moved (6)

**Willingness to pay:** Samsara FY2025 ARR of $1.46 billion (32% YoY) and Motive ARR of $501M (28% YoY) demonstrate that fleet operators will pay substantially for safety platforms that generate behavioral data. The gap is not on the fleet side — it is on the insurer side, where 75% of commercial insurers cite convincing fleets to share telematics data as their biggest barrier, and where 70% of commercial insurers plan UBI program expansion within 1–2 years (GlobeNewswire / telematics survey, n=not specified — treat as directional). Telematics-informed fleets are now securing 15–30% premium reductions, per same source. (7, 8, 9)

**Frequency:**
- 88% of fleets run telematics programs; only 30% share data with insurers (down six points from prior year) — SambaSafety 2025 Annual Telematics Report, n=152 fleet professionals, 180 insurance brokers, 70 carriers, June–July 2025 (10)
- 74% of non-sharing fleets say it's because they were never asked; 75% of commercial insurers say convincing fleets to share is their biggest hurdle (10)
- 82% of commercial insurers now use telematics in their organizations, up from 65% in 2023; only 36% of fleets currently share data (GlobeNewswire, October 2025 — cross-validating the SambaSafety finding directionally) (9)
- 14 consecutive years of commercial auto underwriting losses — the longest sustained loss run in this line in modern US insurance history (1)

**Evidenced or assumed:**
(1) AM Best Market Segment Report "US Commercial Auto Insurance Segment Stuck in Reverse" (Sept 2025) — liability sub-line combined ratio 113 (2024); physical damage sub-line 88.6; blended segment ~107; $4.9B 2024 + ~$5B 2023 = $10B+ cumulative; 14th consecutive year of losses; under-reserved $4–$5B — https://news.ambest.com/newscontent.aspx?AltSrc=45&refnum=269297; cross-validated: https://www.insurancejournal.com/news/national/2025/09/22/840105.htm; https://www.carriermanagement.com/news/2025/09/23/270017.htm; https://riskandinsurance.com/commercial-auto-stuck-in-reverse-am-best/. Full Best's Market Segment Report PDF behind paywall: [NEEDS-ATTENDED-FETCH] https://www3.ambest.com/bestx/crisk/pub/bestsmarketreports.aspx
(2) ATRI 2025 Operational Costs of Trucking — $0.102/mile insurance (auto liability + cargo, ex-physical damage) confirmed for 2024, up 3.0% from $0.099/mile in 2023; $2.260/mile total; auto liability premiums rose 36% per mile over 8 years (ATRI Nov 2025 press release) — https://www.truckinginfo.com/10243311/fleet-margins-fall-as-trucking-costs-set-new-records-in-atris-2025-report; ATRI Nov 2025 press release: https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/new-atri-research-to-examine-the-impacts-of-rising-insurance-costs-on-the-trucking-industry-302315770.html; primary PDF: [NEEDS-ATTENDED-FETCH] https://truckingresearch.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/07/ATRI-Operational-Costs-of-Trucking-07-2025.pdf
(3) Marathon Strategies — 8 trucking-only verdicts, $790.5M; 15 trucking+auto verdicts, $4.1B — https://www.ccjdigital.com/business/article/15770449/trucking-industry-faces-41b-in-megaverdicts; https://marathonstrategies.com/report/corporate-verdicts-go-thermonuclear-2025-edition/
(4) Samsara–Northland Insurance (Travelers) — 10% discount, $300 dashcam subsidy — https://www.samsara.com/blog/northland-insurance-partnership
(5) Motive–GEICO DriveEasy Pro — up to 10% savings — https://www.geico.com/about/pressreleases/2025/20251118/
(6) Nirvana Series C — $80M at $830M valuation (March 2025, General Catalyst leading), $100M+ premiums under management — https://techcrunch.com/2025/03/10/nirvana-keeps-on-truckin-with-80m-at-a-830m-valuation-for-its-ai-powered-insurance/
(7) Samsara FY2025 ARR $1.46B — https://s29.q4cdn.com/853855404/files/doc_financials/2025/q4/Q4-2025-Earnings-Press-Release-Draft-FINAL.pdf
(8) Motive ARR $501M — SEC S-1 — https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1646681/000162828025058773/motive-sx1.htm
(9) GlobeNewswire / telematics adoption survey — 70% insurers plan UBI expansion, 82% use telematics, 36% of fleets share data — https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2024/10/30/2971824/0/en/Telematics-Adoption-Soars-as-70-of-Commercial-Insurers-Plan-UBI-Expansion-Amid-Rising-Fleet-Demand.html
(10) SambaSafety 2025 Annual Telematics Report — 88% fleets have telematics, 30% share data, 74% never asked, n=152 fleet professionals / 180 brokers / 70 carriers — https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2025/10/15/3167243/0/en/88-of-Fleets-Now-Use-Telematics-for-Safety-But-Only-30-Share-Data-with-Insurers.html; https://sambasafety.com/blog/why-fleets-wont-share-telematics-data
(11) HDVI — all insured trucks require telematics + dashcam — https://hdvi.com/fleets/
(12) Nirvana telematics underwriting — 20B trucking miles, 90-day vetting — https://www.nirvanatech.com/why-telematics
(13) ATRI November 2025 announcement — new insurance cost research study with telematics component — https://truckingresearch.org/2025/11/new-atri-research-to-study-rising-commercial-auto-insurance-costs-risk-management-strategies/
(14) FreightWaves on telematics underwriting — https://www.freightwaves.com/news/telematics-on-the-rise-in-trucking-insurance-underwriting

**Root Causes**

- **RC1: Insurers' claims and underwriting departments operate as organizational silos that do not share behavioral data across the policy lifecycle.** Even when a fleet has telematics data available and a claims adjuster reviews a major loss event, the behavioral record is not systematically forwarded to the underwriting team for premium adjustment at renewal. Carrier Management (2025) documents this precisely: claims processes major losses without accessing telematics data; underwriting prices renewals without loss history integration; risk management sends unread safety alerts. This is an organizational design problem, not an IT problem — each insurance department has separate workflows optimized for different objectives, with no cross-department data-coordination requirement. The result is that even the minority of carriers claiming to use telematics in underwriting likely do so inconsistently, not as a systematic renewal input. (Carrier Management: https://www.carriermanagement.com/features/2025/11/24/281755.htm)

- **RC2: Telematics data is generated in dozens of proprietary formats with no normalization standard, making it technically expensive for traditional insurers to ingest at scale.** The commercial trucking market runs Samsara, Motive, Lytx, Netradyne, Verizon Connect, Geotab, Trimble, and hundreds of regional providers, each with proprietary event schemas, scoring models, and API structures. An insurer attempting to underwrite on telematics must build or buy data normalization infrastructure for every provider in its policyholder base. This is feasible for a technology-native startup (Nirvana, HDVI) that builds this as its core product, but prohibitively expensive for a traditional commercial insurer managing a multi-line book of business. No telematics vendor has a commercial incentive to standardize formats because proprietary schemas create switching costs that benefit vendor retention. [ASSUMED-3]: A traditional insurer would need 18–36 months and $5–20M in engineering investment to build a production-grade multi-provider telematics-normalization underwriting layer for 10+ providers — to validate: ask an InsurTech underwriting infrastructure team or a Tier-1 insurer's technology team for build vs. buy estimates.

- **RC3: Commercial auto underwriting uses credibility-weighted actuarial methods that require portfolio-level behavioral data no single fleet's telematics record provides.** Traditional underwriters price using credibility-weighted actuarial methods: a fleet's individual loss history is blended with class-wide loss statistics based on fleet size and years of data. A single fleet's telematics record — even 90 days of dashcam footage — does not have sufficient actuarial credibility to override class-wide pricing at a major insurer. Nirvana solves this by aggregating behavioral data across 20+ billion trucking miles, creating portfolio-level credibility. Traditional insurers have not built equivalent behavioral databases because their underwriting was never designed around continuous behavioral signals. This creates a chicken-and-egg trap: insurers cannot credibly price telematics data until they have actuarial history with it, but cannot build that history without first deploying telematics-based programs. (Nirvana — 20B miles: https://www.nirvanatech.com/why-telematics)

- **RC4: Social inflation (nuclear verdicts) is driving loss severity faster than any behavioral improvement can reduce expected loss, reducing insurer willingness to extend behavioral discounts.** Commercial auto liability posted a 2024 combined ratio of 113 and is estimated under-reserved by $4–5B (AM Best). A single adverse trucking nuclear verdict can exceed a fleet's entire premium contribution regardless of its behavioral record, and no telematics platform captures the variable most predictive of nuclear verdict exposure — plaintiff attorney strategy and jury composition, not driving behavior per se. In this environment, traditional underwriters are less willing to extend large behavioral discounts because the tail risk of a nuclear verdict is not addressable through telematics. This dynamic is structural: tort litigation economics are driving loss severity, and the defense value of dashcam footage (which can cut both ways) does not straightforwardly reduce tail-risk exposure the way frequency reduction does. (AM Best via Insurance Journal: https://www.insurancejournal.com/news/national/2025/09/22/840105.htm; Marsh MMA nuclear verdicts: https://www.marshmma.com/us/insights/details/nuclear-trucking-verdicts.html)

---

### Pain point 3.3

**Description:** ELD compliance, which the FMCSA's December 2019 mandate made mandatory for nearly all CDL interstate motor carriers, rests on a self-certification regime with no independent device testing and an enforcement structure that is structurally outpaced by fraud. ELD manufacturers self-certify compliance by checking a form stating their device meets 49 CFR Part 395 Appendix A specifications; FMCSA has historically not tested devices, validated software, or inspected hardware before registration.
